(187)

\displaystyle L_{\tilde{\Phi}^{(l)}}=L_{\sigma}^{L}\prod_{t=1}^{L}\left(\left\lVert\Delta\Theta_{\Phi_{t}^{(l)}}\right\rVert_{F}+\left\lVert\Theta_{\Phi_{t}^{(l)}}\right\rVert_{F}\right),

(188)

\displaystyle D_{\tilde{\Psi}^{(t)}(0,0)}=\left(\Delta_{\Psi}^{(t)}+1\right)\left\lVert\Psi^{(t)}(0,0)\right\rVert_{\infty},

(189)

\displaystyle D_{\tilde{\Phi}^{(t)}(0,0)}=\left(\Delta_{\Phi}^{(t)}+1\right)\left\lVert\Phi^{(t)}(0,0)\right\rVert_{\infty}.

(190)

Proof.

Applying the continuous pooling operation and cMPNN structure in Def.
B.8
, we expand the following:

\displaystyle\left\lVert\bar{h}_{W,P}(f)-\widetilde{\bar{h}}_{W,P}(f)\right\rVert_{\infty}

\displaystyle=\left\lVert\int_{\mathcal{X}}h_{W,P}^{(T)}\circ f(x)\,dP(x)-\int_{\mathcal{X}}\tilde{h}_{W,P}^{(T)}\circ f(x)\,dP(x)\right\rVert_{\infty}

\displaystyle=\left\lVert\int_{\mathcal{X}}\left(h_{W,P}^{(T)}\circ f(x)-\tilde{h}_{W,P}^{(T)}\circ f(x)\right)\,dP(x)\right\rVert_{\infty}\leq\left\lVert f^{(T)}-\tilde{f}^{(T)}\right\rVert_{\infty}

(191)

where we define
f^{(l+1)}
be the output function of layer
l+1
.
For the
(l+1)
-th layer, it has

\displaystyle\left\lVert f^{(l+1)}-\tilde{f}^{(l+1)}\right\rVert_{\infty}

\displaystyle=\Psi^{(l+1)}\left(f^{(l)},M_{W}^{\Phi^{(l+1)},f^{(l)}}\right)-\tilde{\Psi}^{(l+1)}\left(\tilde{f}^{(l)},M_{W}^{\tilde{\Phi}^{(l+1)},\tilde{f}^{(l)}}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\underbrace{\left\lVert\Psi^{(l+1)}\left(f^{(l)},M_{W}^{\Phi^{(l+1)},f^{(l)}}\right)-\Psi^{(l+1)}\left(\tilde{f}^{(l)},M_{W}^{\Phi^{(l+1)},f^{(l)}}\right)\right\rVert_{\infty}}_{T_{1}}

\displaystyle+\underbrace{\left\lVert\Psi^{(l+1)}\left(\tilde{f}^{(l)},M_{W}^{\Phi^{(l+1)},f^{(l)}}\right)-\Psi^{(l+1)}\left(\tilde{f}^{(l)},M_{W}^{\tilde{\Phi}^{(l+1)},f^{(l)}}\right)\right\rVert_{\infty}}_{T_{2}}

\displaystyle+\underbrace{\left\lVert\Psi^{(l+1)}\left(\tilde{f}^{(l)},M_{W}^{\tilde{\Phi}^{(l+1)},f^{(l)}}\right)-\Psi^{(l+1)}\left(\tilde{f}^{(l)},M_{W}^{\tilde{\Phi}^{(l+1)},\tilde{f}^{(l)}}\right)\right\rVert_{\infty}}_{T_{3}}

\displaystyle+\underbrace{\left\lVert\Psi^{(l+1)}\left(\tilde{f}^{(l)},M_{W}^{\tilde{\Phi}^{(l+1)},\tilde{f}^{(l)}}\right)-\tilde{\Psi}^{(l+1)}\left(\tilde{f}^{(l)},M_{W}^{\tilde{\Phi}^{(l+1)},\tilde{f}^{(l)}}\right)\right\rVert_{\infty}}_{T_{4}}.

(192)

Next, we bound each of the terms
\{T_{i}\}_{i=1}^{4}
separately using results from Thm.
J.7
.

The second input variables of the two functions in
T_{1}
are identical. Thus, it has

T_{1}\leq L_{\Psi^{(l+1)}}\left\lVert f^{(l)}-\tilde{f}^{(l)}\right\rVert_{\infty}.

(193)

The first input variables of the two functions in
T_{2}
are identical, thus we have

\displaystyle T_{2}

\displaystyle\leq L_{\Psi^{(l+1)}}\left\lVert\int_{\mathcal{X}}\frac{W(x,y)}{d_{W}(x)}\Phi^{(l+1)}\left(f^{(l)}(x),f^{(l)}(y)\right)\,dP(y)-\right.

\displaystyle\left.\int_{\mathcal{X}}\frac{W(x,y)}{d_{W}(x)}\tilde{\Phi}^{(l+1)}\left(f^{(l)}(x),f^{(l)}(y)\right)\,dP(y)\right\rVert_{\infty}

\displaystyle\leq L_{\Psi^{(l+1)}}\frac{\lVert W\rVert_{\infty}}{d_{\min}}\int_{\mathcal{X}}\left\lVert\Phi^{(l+1)}\left(f^{(l)}(x),f^{(l)}(y)\right)-\tilde{\Phi}^{(l+1)}\left(f^{(l)}(x),f^{(l)}(y)\right)\right\rVert_{\infty}\,dP(y).

(194)

Applying norm inequality
\|v\|_{\infty}\leq\|v\|_{2}\leq\sqrt{d}\|v\|_{\infty}
for
v\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
and Thm.
J.7
, it has

\displaystyle T_{2}

\displaystyle\leq L_{\Psi^{(l+1)}}\frac{\lVert W\rVert_{\infty}}{d_{\min}}\int_{\mathcal{X}}\left\lVert\Phi^{(l+1)}\left(f^{(l)}(x),f^{(l)}(y)\right)-\tilde{\Phi}^{(l+1)}\left(f^{(l)}(x),f^{(l)}(y)\right)\right\rVert\,dP(y)

\displaystyle\leq L_{\Psi^{(l+1)}}\frac{\lVert W\rVert_{\infty}}{d_{\min}}\left(\frac{\beta}{\alpha}\kappa^{2}\right)^{L}\left[\prod_{k=1}^{L}\left(1+\frac{\left\lVert\Delta\Theta_{\Phi_{k}^{(l+1)}}\right\rVert_{F}}{\left\lVert\Theta_{\Phi_{k}^{(l+1)}}\right\rVert_{F}}\right)-1\right]

\displaystyle\cdot\int_{\mathcal{X}}\left\lVert\Phi^{(l+1)}\left(f^{(l)}(x),f^{(l)}(y)\right)\right\rVert\,dP(y)

\displaystyle\leq L_{\Psi^{(l+1)}}\frac{\lVert W\rVert_{\infty}}{d_{\min}}\Delta_{\Phi}^{(l+1)}\int_{\mathcal{X}}\left\lVert\Phi^{(l+1)}\left(f^{(l)}(x),f^{(l)}(y)\right)\right\rVert_{\infty}\,dP(y)

\displaystyle\leq L_{\Psi^{(l+1)}}\frac{\lVert W\rVert_{\infty}}{d_{\min}}\Delta_{\Phi}^{(l+1)}\int_{\mathcal{X}}\left(L_{\Phi^{(l+1)}}\left\lVert f^{(l)}(y)\right\rVert_{\infty}+\lVert\Phi^{(l+1)}(0,0)\rVert_{\infty}\right)\,dP(y).

(195)

Further applying the result from Lem.
F.3
as in Eq. (
108
), it has

\displaystyle T_{2}

\displaystyle\leq L_{\Psi^{(l+1)}}\frac{\lVert W\rVert_{\infty}}{d_{\min}}\Delta_{\Phi}^{(l+1)}\int_{\mathcal{X}}\left(L_{\Phi^{(l+1)}}\left(C_{1}^{(l)}+C_{2}^{(l)}\lVert f\rVert_{\infty}\right)+\lVert\Phi^{(l+1)}(0,0)\rVert_{\infty}\right)\,dP(y)

\displaystyle=L_{\Psi^{(l+1)}}\lVert W\rVert_{\infty}\Delta_{\Phi}^{(l+1)}C_{\Phi}^{(l+1)}d_{\min}^{-1}=C_{T_{2}}^{(l+1)}.

(196)

To bound
T_{3}
, a similar strategy is applied, and it has

\displaystyle T_{3}

\displaystyle\leq L_{\Psi^{(l+1)}}\left\lVert\int_{\mathcal{X}}\frac{W(x,y)}{d_{W}(x)}\left[\tilde{\Phi}^{(l+1)}\left(f^{(l)}(x),f^{(l)}(y)\right)-\tilde{\Phi}^{(l+1)}\left(\tilde{f}^{(l)}(x),\tilde{f}^{(l)}(y)\right)\right]\,dP(y)\right\rVert_{\infty}

\displaystyle\leq L_{\Psi^{(l+1)}}\frac{\lVert W\rVert_{\infty}}{d_{\min}}L_{\tilde{\Phi}}^{(l+1)}\left\lVert f^{(l)}-\tilde{f}^{(l)}\right\rVert_{\infty}.

(197)

Note that
L_{\tilde{\Phi}^{(l+1)}}
in Eq. (
G
) is unknown due to the change of neural network weights of
\Phi^{(l+1)}
. We now seek to bound this new Lipschitz constant after weight perturbation.
Both
\Psi
and
\Phi
are assumed to be
L
-layer MLPs described by
\circ_{t=1}^{L}\left(\sigma_{t}\circ W_{t}(x)\right)
as in Lemma
E.3
, where the Lipschitz constant of the used activation function is denoted by
L_{\sigma}
.
According to the result from Lemma
E.3
as in Eq. (
69
), the Lipschitz constant of such an MLP is bounded by

L_{\textmd{MLP}}=L_{\sigma}^{L}\prod_{t=1}^{L}\lambda_{t}\leq L_{\sigma}^{L}\prod_{t=1}^{L}\|W_{t}\|_{F},

(198)

where the last inequality results from the fact that the maximum eigenvalue of a matrix is bounded by its Frobenius norm.
After the weight perturbation
\Delta W_{t}=\tilde{W}_{t}-W_{t}
, it has

\tilde{L}_{\textmd{MLP}}\leq L_{\sigma}^{L}\prod_{t=1}^{L}\left\|\tilde{W}_{t}\right\|_{F}=L_{\sigma}^{L}\prod_{t=1}^{L}\|W_{t}+\Delta W_{t}\|_{F}\leq L_{\sigma}^{L}\prod_{t=1}^{L}\left(\|\Delta W_{t}\|_{F}+\|W_{t}\|_{F}\right).

(199)

Applying the above result over the perturbed MLPs of
\Psi^{(l)}
and
\Phi^{(l)}
, it has

\displaystyle L_{\tilde{\Psi}^{(l)}}\leq\;

\displaystyle L_{\sigma}^{L}\prod_{t=1}^{L}\left(\left\lVert\Delta\Theta_{\Psi_{t}^{(l)}}\right\rVert_{F}+\left\lVert\Theta_{\Psi_{t}^{(l)}}\right\rVert_{F}\right),

(200)

\displaystyle L_{\tilde{\Phi}^{(l)}}\leq\;

\displaystyle L_{\sigma}^{L}\prod_{t=1}^{L}\left(\left\lVert\Delta\Theta_{\Phi_{t}^{(l)}}\right\rVert_{F}+\left\lVert\Theta_{\Phi_{t}^{(l)}}\right\rVert_{F}\right).

(201)

Incorporating Eq. (
201
) into Eq. (
G
), we can conclude an upper bound of
T_{3}
:

T_{3}\leq L_{\Psi^{(l+1)}}\frac{\lVert W\rVert_{\infty}}{d_{\min}}L_{\sigma}^{L}\prod_{t=1}^{L}\left(\left\lVert\Delta\Theta_{\Phi_{t}^{(l)}}\right\rVert_{F}+\left\lVert\Theta_{\Phi_{t}^{(l)}}\right\rVert_{F}\right)\left\lVert f^{(l)}-\tilde{f}^{(l)}\right\rVert_{\infty}=C_{T_{3}}^{(l+1)}\left\lVert f^{(l)}-\tilde{f}^{(l)}\right\rVert_{\infty}.

(202)

We use Thm.
J.7
, as well as norm inequalities, to bound
T_{4}
:

\displaystyle T_{4}

\displaystyle\leq\left\lVert\Psi^{(l+1)}\left(\tilde{f}^{(l)},M_{W}^{\tilde{\Phi}^{(l+1)},\tilde{f}^{(l)}}\right)-\tilde{\Psi}^{(l+1)}\left(\tilde{f}^{(l)},M_{W}^{\tilde{\Phi}^{(l+1)},\tilde{f}^{(l)}}\right)\right\rVert

\displaystyle\leq\Delta_{\Psi}^{(l+1)}\left\lVert\Psi^{(l+1)}\left(\tilde{f}^{(l)},M_{W}^{\tilde{\Phi}^{(l+1)},\tilde{f}^{(l)}}\right)\right\rVert_{\infty}

\displaystyle\leq\Delta_{\Psi}^{(l+1)}\left(\left\lVert\Psi^{(l+1)}\left(\tilde{f}^{(l)},M_{W}^{\tilde{\Phi}^{(l+1)},\tilde{f}^{(l)}}\right)-\Psi^{(l+1)}(0,0)\right\rVert_{\infty}+\left\lVert\Psi^{(l+1)}(0,0)\right\rVert_{\infty}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\Delta_{\Psi}^{(l+1)}L_{\Psi^{(l+1)}}\max\left(\left\lVert\tilde{f}^{(l)}\right\rVert_{\infty},\left\lVert\int_{\mathcal{X}}\frac{W(x,y)}{d_{W}(x)}\tilde{\Phi}^{(l+1)}\left(\tilde{f}^{(l)}(x),\tilde{f}^{(l)}(y)\right)\,dP(y)\right\rVert_{\infty}\right)

\displaystyle+\Delta_{\Psi}^{(l+1)}\left\lVert\Psi^{(l+1)}(0,0)\right\rVert_{\infty}

\displaystyle\leq\Delta_{\Psi}^{(l+1)}L_{\Psi^{(l+1)}}\max\left(\left\lVert\tilde{f}^{(l)}\right\rVert_{\infty},\frac{\left\lVert W\right\rVert_{\infty}}{d_{\min}}\left\lVert\tilde{\Phi}^{(l+1)}\left(\tilde{f}^{(l)},\tilde{f}^{(l)}\right)\right\rVert_{\infty}\right)+\Delta_{\Psi}^{(l+1)}\left\lVert\Psi^{(l+1)}(0,0)\right\rVert_{\infty},

(203)

We further bound the term
\left\lVert\tilde{\Phi}^{(l+1)}\left(\tilde{f}^{(l)},\tilde{f}^{(l)}\right)\right\rVert_{\infty}
in the above equation as follows:

\displaystyle\left\lVert\tilde{\Phi}^{(l+1)}\left(\tilde{f}^{(l)},\tilde{f}^{(l)}\right)\right\rVert_{\infty}

\displaystyle\leq\;

\displaystyle\left\lVert\tilde{\Phi}^{(l+1)}\left(\tilde{f}^{(l)},\tilde{f}^{(l)}\right)-\Phi^{(l+1)}\left(\tilde{f}^{(l)},\tilde{f}^{(l)}\right)\right\rVert_{\infty}+\left\lVert\Phi^{(l+1)}\left(\tilde{f}^{(l)},\tilde{f}^{(l)}\right)\right\rVert_{\infty}

\displaystyle\leq\;

\displaystyle\Delta_{\Phi}^{(l+1)}\left\lVert\Phi^{(l+1)}\left(\tilde{f}^{(l)},\tilde{f}^{(l)}\right)\right\rVert_{\infty}+\left\lVert\Phi^{(l+1)}\left(\tilde{f}^{(l)},\tilde{f}^{(l)}\right)\right\rVert_{\infty}

\displaystyle\leq\;

\displaystyle\left(\Delta_{\Phi}^{(l+1)}+1\right)\left(L_{\Phi^{(l+1)}}\left\lVert\tilde{f}^{(l)}\right\rVert_{\infty}+\left\lVert\Phi^{(l+1)}(0,0)\right\rVert_{\infty}\right).

(204)

Both Eq. (
G
) and (
G
) contain the term
\lVert\tilde{f}^{(l)}\rVert_{\infty}
, which is further bounded by applying the result from Lem.
F.3
as in Eq. (
108
), as

\left\lVert\tilde{f}^{(l)}\right\rVert_{\infty}\leq\tilde{C_{1}}^{(l)}+\tilde{C_{2}}^{(l)}\left\lVert\tilde{f}^{(0)}\right\rVert_{\infty}=\tilde{C_{1}}^{(l)}+\tilde{C_{2}}^{(l)}\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{\infty},

(205)

where

\displaystyle\tilde{C_{1}}^{(l)}

\displaystyle=\sum_{t=1}^{l}\left(L_{\tilde{\Psi}^{(t)}}\left\lVert\tilde{\Phi}^{(t)}(0,0)\right\rVert_{\infty}+\left\lVert\tilde{\Psi}^{(t)}(0,0)\right\rVert_{\infty}\right)\prod_{l^{\prime}=t+1}^{l}L_{\tilde{\Psi}^{(l^{\prime})}}\left(1+L_{\tilde{\Phi}^{(l^{\prime})}}\right),

(206)

\displaystyle\tilde{C_{2}}^{(l)}

\displaystyle=\prod_{t=1}^{l}L_{\tilde{\Psi}^{(t)}}\left(1+L_{\tilde{\Phi}^{(t)}}\right).

(207)

Applying Eq. (
G
), it has

\left\lVert\tilde{\Phi}^{(t)}(0,0)\right\rVert_{\infty}\leq\left(\Delta_{\Phi}^{(t)}+1\right)\left\lVert\Phi^{(t)}(0,0)\right\rVert_{\infty}.\\

(208)

Similarly, we can derive the same result for
\Psi
, as

\left\lVert\tilde{\Psi}^{(t)}(0,0)\right\rVert_{\infty}\leq\left(\Delta_{\Psi}^{(t)}+1\right)\left\lVert\Psi^{(t)}(0,0)\right\rVert_{\infty}.\\

(209)

The constant
\tilde{C_{1}}^{(l)}
can be inflated, expressed in terms of the original network before perturbation:
Also, the Lipschitz constants
L_{\tilde{\Phi}^{(t)}}
and
L_{\tilde{\Psi}^{(t)}}
are upper bounded by Eq. (
201
) and Eq. (
200
) respectively. These result in further inflated expression of
\tilde{C_{1}}^{(l)}
and
\tilde{C_{2}}^{(l)}
as in the theorem body.
Substituting Eq. (
205
) back to Eq. (
G
) and then Eq. (
G
), we obtain the following:

T_{4}\leq C_{T_{4}}^{(l+1)}.

(210)

Finally, combining the upper bounds of
\{T_{i}\}_{i=1}^{4}
, as in Eq. (
193
), (
G
), (
202
), (
210
), we can conclude the following upper bound for Eq. (
G
):

\displaystyle\left\lVert f^{(l+1)}-\tilde{f}^{(l+1)}\right\rVert_{\infty}

\displaystyle\leq L_{\Psi^{(l+1)}}\left\lVert f^{(l)}-\tilde{f}^{(l)}\right\rVert_{\infty}+C_{T_{2}}^{(l+1)}+C_{T_{3}}^{(l+1)}\left\lVert f^{(l)}-\tilde{f}^{(l)}\right\rVert_{\infty}+C_{T_{4}}^{(l+1)}

\displaystyle=\left(L_{\Psi^{(l+1)}}+C_{T_{3}}^{(l+1)}\right)\left\lVert f^{(l)}-\tilde{f}^{(l)}\right\rVert_{\infty}+C_{T_{2}}^{(l+1)}+C_{T_{4}}^{(l+1)}.

(211)

Applying the recursive result from Lem.
F.1
to the above Eq. (
G
) and recalling that
\lVert f^{(0)}-\tilde{f}^{(0)}\rVert_{\infty}=0
, we have:

\displaystyle\left\lVert f^{(l+1)}-\tilde{f}^{(l+1)}\right\rVert_{\infty}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{l=1}^{T}\left(C_{T_{2}}^{(l)}+C_{T_{4}}^{(l)}\right)\prod_{l^{\prime}=l+1}^{T}\left(L_{\Psi^{(l^{\prime})}}+C_{T_{3}}^{(l^{\prime})}\right).

(212)

Substituting Eq. (
212
) back to Eq. (
G
), this completes the proof.

∎

Theorem G.5

(Optimization Error)
.

Given an RGM
\Gamma=(W,P,f)
and the discrete and continuous versions of an MPNN as in Def.
B.7
and Def.
B.8
, where the message and update functions are instantiated as
L
-layer MLPs as in Def
B.9
.
Perturb the RGM by both kernel and distribution deformation.
Perturb the MPNN weight matrices by Def.
B.10
.
Suppose Assumptions
C.1
-
C.3
,
C.5
-
C.7
and
C.9
hold.
Define an additional constant

\tilde{C_{3}}^{(T)}:=\bigg(\frac{1}{d_{\min}}+\frac{W_{\max}}{d_{\min}^{2}}\bigg)\sum_{l=1}^{T}L_{\tilde{\Psi}^{(l)}}\left(L_{\tilde{\Phi}^{(l)}}\left(\tilde{C_{1}}^{(l-1)}+\tilde{C_{2}}^{(l-1)}\lVert f\rVert_{\infty}\right)+D_{\tilde{\Phi}^{(t)}(0,0)}\right).

(213)

Then, the hypothesis change induced by RGM deformation and weight perturbation is bounded by the following:

\left\lVert\bar{h}_{W,P}(f)-\tilde{\bar{h}}_{W_{\tau},P_{\tau}}(f)\right\rVert_{\infty}\leq\Delta_{\Gamma,\Theta}

(214)

where

\Delta_{\Gamma,\Theta}=\sum_{l=1}^{T}\left(C_{T_{2}}^{(l)}+C_{T_{4}}^{(l)}\right)\prod_{l^{\prime}=l+1}^{T}L_{\Psi^{(l^{\prime})}}\left(1+C_{T_{3}}^{(l^{\prime})}\right)+\tilde{C_{3}}^{(T)}C_{\nabla w}\lVert\nabla\tau\rVert_{\infty}+\left(\tilde{C_{1}}^{(T)}+\tilde{C_{2}}^{(T)}\lVert f\rVert_{\infty}\right)N_{P_{\tau}}.

(215)

Proof.

Combining results from Theorems
G.3
and
G.4
, we upper bound the hypothesis change induced by both RGM deformation and network weight perturbation, as below:

\displaystyle\left\lVert\bar{h}_{W,P}(f)-\widetilde{\bar{h}}_{W_{\tau},P_{\tau}}(f)\right\rVert_{\infty}

(216)

\displaystyle\leq\;

\displaystyle\left\lVert\bar{h}_{W,P}(f)-\widetilde{\bar{h}}_{W,P}(f)\right\rVert_{\infty}+\left\lVert\widetilde{\bar{h}}_{W,P}(f)-\widetilde{\bar{h}}_{W_{\tau},P_{\tau}}(f)\right\rVert_{\infty}

\displaystyle\leq\;

\displaystyle\sum_{l=1}^{T}\left(C_{T_{2}}^{(l)}+C_{T_{4}}^{(l)}\right)\prod_{l^{\prime}=l+1}^{T}\left(L_{\Psi^{(l^{\prime})}}+C_{T_{3}}^{(l^{\prime})}\right)+\tilde{C_{3}}^{(T)}C_{\nabla w}\lVert\nabla\tau\rVert_{\infty}+\left(\tilde{C_{1}}^{(T)}+\tilde{C_{2}}^{(T)}\lVert f\rVert_{\infty}\right)N_{P_{\tau}}.

where

\tilde{C_{3}}^{(T)}:=\bigg(\frac{1}{d_{\min}}+\frac{W_{\max}}{d_{\min}^{2}}\bigg)\sum_{l=1}^{T}L_{\tilde{\Psi}^{(l)}}\left(L_{\tilde{\Phi}^{(l)}}\left(\tilde{C_{1}}^{(l-1)}+\tilde{C_{2}}^{(l-1)}\lVert f\rVert_{\infty}\right)+\left\lVert\Phi^{(l)}(0,0)\right\rVert_{\infty}\right).

(217)

Applying the upper bounds derived for the Lipschitz constants for the perturbed networks as in Eqs. (
200
) and (
201
), we re-define the above constant as in Eq. (
213
).
This completes the proof.

∎

Appendix H
Proof Theorem
3.1

We present our complete main result in the following, which is proved by combining key results from Prop.
2.2
, Lem.
E.2
, Lem.
E.5
, Thm.
F.5
and Thm.
G.5
.

Theorem H.1

(Main Result in Theorem
3.1
)
.

Following definitions in Appendix
B
and suppose assumptions in Appendix
C
hold. Suppose Lem.
E.2
holds with probability
1-\rho
, and events
\mathcal{E}_{1},\mathcal{E}_{2}
defined in Lemma
F.2
each hold with probability at least
1-\rho
. Then, the following holds with a probability at least
1-3\rho
:

\displaystyle\epsilon_{T}(h,g_{D})\leq\epsilon_{S}(h,g_{D})+\sqrt{\frac{C\Delta_{D}}{\lambda_{r}}}\left(L_{NN}\left(\sqrt{\Delta_{N}}+\Delta_{\Gamma,\Theta}+\varepsilon_{3}+\varepsilon_{4}\right)+G_{NN}\right),

(218)

where
\Delta_{D},\Delta_{N},\Delta_{\Gamma,\Theta}
are computed by Eq. (
8
), (
12
), (
15
).

Proof.

By Prop.
2.2
and
\mathcal{W}_{1}(\mu_{S},\mu_{T})\leq\mathcal{W}_{2}(\mu_{S},\mu_{T})
, we have:

\epsilon_{T}(h,g_{D})\leq\epsilon_{S}(h,g_{D})+\left\lVert\ell_{h,g_{D}}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{H}_{K_{\ell}}}\cdot\mathcal{W}_{2}(\mu_{S},\mu_{T}).

(219)

By Lem.
E.2
, it has
\mathcal{W}_{2}^{2}(\mu_{S},\mu_{T})\leq\Delta_{D}
, where the domain divergence upper bound
\Delta_{D}
is given in Eq. (
53
).
Substituting the above, together with the Lem.
E.5
result in Eq. (
77
) and the error decomposition result in Eq. (
11
), back to Eq. (
219
), it has

\displaystyle\epsilon_{T}(h,g_{D})\leq\;

\displaystyle\epsilon_{S}(h,g_{D})+\sqrt{\frac{C\Delta_{D}}{\lambda_{r}}}\left(L_{NN}\underbrace{\left\|\bar{h}_{G}(Z)-\bar{h}_{W,P}(f)\right\|_{\infty}}_{\textmd{convergence error}}+\right.

\displaystyle\left.L_{NN}\underbrace{\left\|\bar{h}_{W,P}(f)-\widetilde{\bar{h}}_{W_{\tau},P_{\tau}}(f)\right\|_{\infty}}_{\textmd{optimization error}}+L_{NN}\varepsilon_{3}+L_{NN}\varepsilon_{4}+G_{NN}\right).

(220)

By substituting the upper bounds of the convergence error and optimization error derived in Thm.
F.5
and Thm.
G.5
, respectively, we conclude the final generalization DA bound.
This completes the proof.
∎

Appendix I
Proof of Corollary
3.2

In this section, we further analyze the result of Theorem
3.1
, examining key factors that affect the bound tightness, for the case when the formal bias of the MPNN message and update functions is zero, i.e.,
\lVert\Phi^{(t)}(0,0)\rVert_{\infty},\lVert\Psi^{(t)}(0,0)\rVert_{\infty}=0
and when approximation and label error is zero, i.e.,
\varepsilon_{3},\varepsilon_{4}=0
.
Our analysis can be easily extended to cases with nonzero formal bias and to keep
\varepsilon_{3}
and
\varepsilon_{4}
in the result.

Before proceeding further, we introduce a few new notations.
Denote the maximum number of graph instances among all classes by
m_{S}
for the source domain and
m_{T}
for the target domain, and define a hybrid sample size resulting from both graph number and node number as
M=\max(N_{S}m_{S},N_{T}m_{T})
.
Define the maximum domain shift by
\mathcal{W}_{2}=\arg\max_{j=1}^{C}\mathcal{W}_{2}\left(\hat{P}_{S}^{j},\hat{P}_{T}^{j}\right)
.
For the MLP classifier, define two quantities relevant to its Lipschitz constants of the activation functions and its maximum singular vectors of its weight matrices, i.e.,
L_{\sigma}=\max_{l=1}^{L}L_{\sigma_{l}}
and
\lambda_{M}=\max_{l=1}^{L}\lambda_{l}
.
For the MPNN message and update functions, define a quantity relevant to their Lipschitz constants, i.e.,
L_{P}=\max_{t=1}^{T}\max\left(L_{\Phi^{(t)}},L_{\Psi^{(t)}}\right)
.
For the labeling function that is modeled as a perturbed version of a given hypothesis, define the following quantities relevant to the perturbation strength achieved through changing neural network weights:

\displaystyle\Delta_{M}=\max_{l=1}^{L}\frac{\lVert\Delta W_{l}\rVert_{F}}{\lVert W_{l}\rVert_{F}},

(221)

\displaystyle\Delta_{\Theta}=\max_{t=1}^{T}\max_{l=1}^{L}\max\left(\frac{\left\lVert\Delta\Theta_{\Psi_{l}^{(t)}}\right\rVert_{F}}{\left\lVert\Theta_{\Psi_{l}^{(t)}}\right\rVert_{F}},\frac{\left\lVert\Delta\Theta_{\Phi_{l}^{(t)}}\right\rVert_{F}}{\left\lVert\Theta_{\Phi_{l}^{(t)}}\right\rVert_{F}}\right),

(222)

\displaystyle D_{\Theta}=\max_{t=1}^{T}\max_{l=1}^{L}\max\left(\left\lVert\Theta_{\Psi_{l}^{(t)}}\right\rVert_{F},\left\lVert\Theta_{\Phi_{l}^{(t)}}\right\rVert_{F}\right).

(223)

To present the result in a neat fashion, define the following set of quantities computed from properties of the RGM, MLP classifier and MPNN feature extractor:

\displaystyle U_{L_{NN}}=L_{\sigma}^{L}\lambda_{M}^{L},

(224)

\displaystyle U_{G_{NN}}=\left(\frac{\beta}{\alpha}\kappa^{2}\right)^{L}\left(\sum_{k=1}^{L}\binom{L}{k}\Delta_{M}^{k}\right),

(225)

\displaystyle U_{K_{6}}=2TL_{P}^{T+2}(1+L_{P})^{T}L_{W}^{\infty}\left(\sqrt{1+8\lVert W\rVert_{\infty}^{2}L_{P}^{2}d_{\min}^{-2}}\right)^{T-1}d_{\min}^{-1},

\displaystyle U_{K_{7}}=2TL_{P}^{2}\lVert W\rVert_{\infty}\left(1+\lVert W\rVert_{\infty}d_{\min}^{-1}L_{P}\right)d_{\min}^{-1}\left(1+8\lVert W\rVert_{\infty}^{2}L_{P}^{2}d_{\min}^{-2}\right)^{\frac{T-1}{2}},

\displaystyle U_{K_{9}}=0.8TL_{P}^{T+2}(1+L_{P})^{T}C_{\mathcal{X}}\lVert W\rVert_{\infty}\left(\sqrt{1+8\lVert W\rVert_{\infty}^{2}L_{P}^{2}d_{\min}^{-2}}\right)^{T-1}d_{\min}^{-1},

(226)

\displaystyle U_{R_{3}}=56T^{2}L_{P}^{2T+4}(1+L_{P})^{2T}\left(1+8\lVert W\rVert_{\infty}^{2}L_{P}^{2}d_{\min}^{-2}\right)^{T-1}(L_{W}^{\infty})^{2}\left(\sqrt{\log(C_{\mathcal{X}})}+\sqrt{D_{\mathcal{X}}}\right)^{2}\lVert W\rVert_{\infty}^{2}d_{\min}^{-4},

(227)

\displaystyle U_{R_{4}}=56TL_{P}^{2T+4}(1+L_{P})^{2T}\left(\sqrt{2}\left\lVert W\right\rVert_{\infty}+L_{W}^{\infty}\right)^{2}\lVert W\rVert_{\infty}^{2}\left(1+8\lVert W\rVert_{\infty}^{2}L_{P}^{2}d_{\min}^{-2}\right)^{T-1}d_{\min}^{-4},

(228)

\displaystyle U_{S_{2}}=14U_{K_{6}}^{2}+14U_{K_{9}}^{2}\log(C_{\mathcal{X}})+56\left(1+\lVert W\rVert_{\infty}d_{\min}^{-1}L_{P}\right)^{2}+7L_{P}^{2l}(1+L_{P})^{2l}C_{{\mathcal{X}}}^{2}\log(C_{\mathcal{X}}),

\displaystyle U_{S_{3}}=14U_{K_{7}}^{2}+56\left(1+\lVert W\rVert_{\infty}d_{\min}^{-1}L_{P}\right)^{2}L_{f}^{2},

\displaystyle U_{S_{5}}=14U_{K_{9}}^{2}+7C_{\mathcal{X}}^{2}L_{P}^{2l}(1+L_{P})^{2l},

\displaystyle U_{T_{2}}=\left(14U_{K_{9}}^{2}+7C_{\mathcal{X}}^{2}L_{P}^{2l}(1+L_{P})^{2l}\right)\frac{D_{\mathcal{X}}}{2(D_{\mathcal{X}}+1)},

(229)

\displaystyle Q_{1}=U_{T_{2}}\lVert f\rVert_{\infty}^{2}+1.5\left(U_{S_{2}}\lVert f\rVert_{\infty}^{2}+U_{S_{3}}L_{f}^{2}\right),

(230)

\displaystyle Q_{2}=1.5U_{S_{5}}\lVert f\rVert_{\infty}^{2}+U_{R_{3}}+0.5U_{R_{4}}\lVert f\rVert_{\infty}^{2},

(231)

\displaystyle Q_{3}=\max\left(L_{\sigma}^{L}D_{\Theta}^{L}\left(1+\Delta_{\Theta}\right)^{L},\left(L_{\sigma}^{L}D_{\Theta}^{L}\left(1+\Delta_{\Theta}\right)^{L}\right)^{T}\right),

(232)

\displaystyle U_{\tilde{C_{3}}^{(T)}}=\frac{d_{\min}+W_{\max}}{d_{\min}^{2}}Q_{3}\left(1+L_{\sigma}^{L}D_{\Theta}^{L}\left(1+\Delta_{\Theta}\right)^{L}\right)^{T+2}\lVert f\rVert_{\infty},

(233)

\displaystyle Q^{(l)}=\left(L_{\sigma}^{L}D_{\Theta}^{L}\left(1+\Delta_{\Theta}\right)^{L}\right)^{l}\left(1+L_{\sigma}^{L}D_{\Theta}^{L}\left(1+\Delta_{\Theta}\right)^{L}\right)^{l}\lVert f\rVert_{\infty},

(234)

\displaystyle U_{C_{5}^{(l+1)}}=Q^{(l)}\max\left(1,\frac{\left\lVert W\right\rVert_{\infty}}{d_{\min}}\left(\sqrt{H_{l+1}}\left(\frac{\beta\kappa^{2}}{\alpha}\right)^{L}\left(\sum_{k=1}^{L}\binom{L}{k}\Delta_{\Theta}^{k}\right)+1\right)L_{P}\right).

(235)

and

\displaystyle U_{C_{T_{2}}^{(l+1)}}=\sqrt{H_{l+1}}\left(\frac{\beta\kappa^{2}}{\alpha}\right)^{L}L_{P}^{l+2}(1+L_{P})^{l}\frac{\lVert W\rVert_{\infty}\lVert f\rVert_{\infty}}{d_{\min}},

(236)

\displaystyle U_{C_{T_{3}}^{(l+1)}}=L_{P}L_{\sigma}^{L}D_{\Theta}^{L}\left(1+\Delta_{\Theta}\right)^{L}\frac{\lVert W\rVert_{\infty}}{d_{\min}},

(237)

\displaystyle U_{C_{T_{4}}^{(l+1)}}=\sqrt{F_{l+1}}\left(\frac{\beta\kappa^{2}}{\alpha}\right)^{L}L_{P}U_{C_{5}^{(l+1)}},

(238)

\displaystyle Q_{4}=\sum_{l=1}^{T}\left(U_{C_{T_{2}}^{(l)}}+U_{C_{T_{4}}^{(l)}}\right)\prod_{l^{\prime}=l+1}^{T}\left(1+U_{C_{T_{3}}^{(l^{\prime})}}\right),

(239)

In the following, we present conditions on graph size (
N
) and the difference between the given hypothesis and labeling function characterized through
\Delta_{M}^{k}
,
\Delta_{\Theta}
and
N_{P_{\tau}}
, and reveal connections how these conditions are linked to properties of the RGM, MLP classifier and MPNN feature extractor.

Corollary I.1

(Sufficient Condition)
.

Follow the same problem setting and assumptions of Theorem
3.1
. Suppose
M\geq\log_{\frac{D_{\mathcal{X}}}{4}}\left(\frac{\left(1+\log(1/\rho)^{\frac{1}{4}}\right)}{0.1\times 27^{\frac{D_{\mathcal{X}}}{4}}}\right)
and
D_{\mathcal{X}}>1
. Then, it has

\displaystyle\sqrt{\Delta_{D}}\leq\sqrt{2L_{2}}CL_{f}\bigg(\mathcal{W}_{2}\left(\hat{P}_{S}^{m},\hat{P}_{T}^{m}\right)+2B\cdot 1.1\times 27^{\frac{D_{\mathcal{X}}}{4}}\bigg)^{\alpha}=U_{\sqrt{\Delta_{D}}},

(240)

\displaystyle\Delta_{N}\leq\frac{\log(N)}{N^{\frac{1}{D_{\mathcal{X}}+1}}}\left(Q_{1}+Q_{2}\log\left(\frac{2}{\rho}\right)\right)=U_{\Delta_{N}},

(241)

\displaystyle\Delta_{\Gamma,\Theta}\leq L_{P}Q_{3}C_{\nabla w}\lVert\nabla\tau\rVert_{\infty}\left(\sum_{k=1}^{L}\binom{L}{k}\Delta_{\Theta}^{k}\right)+U_{\tilde{C_{3}}^{(T)}}C_{\nabla w}\lVert\nabla\tau\rVert_{\infty}+Q^{(T)}N_{P_{\tau}}=U_{\Delta_{\Gamma,\Theta}}.

(242)

For
\xi>0
, define a quantity

\eta=\left(\frac{\xi\epsilon_{S}(h,g_{D})\sqrt{\lambda_{r}}}{\sqrt{2L_{2}}C^{1.5}L_{f}\bigg(\mathcal{W}_{2}\left(\hat{P}_{S}^{m},\hat{P}_{T}^{m}\right)+2B\cdot 1.1\times 27^{\frac{D_{\mathcal{X}}}{4}}\bigg)^{\alpha}}-U_{G_{NN}}\right)L_{\sigma}^{-L}\lambda_{M}^{-L}.

(243)

Supposing the following holds,

\displaystyle\sum_{k=1}^{L}\binom{L}{k}\Delta_{M}^{k}\leq\frac{\xi\epsilon_{S}(h,g_{D})\sqrt{\lambda_{r}}}{\sqrt{2L_{2}}C^{1.5}L_{f}\bigg(\mathcal{W}_{2}\left(\hat{P}_{S}^{m},\hat{P}_{T}^{m}\right)+2B\cdot 1.1\times 27^{\frac{D_{\mathcal{X}}}{4}}\bigg)^{\alpha}\left(\frac{\beta}{\alpha}\kappa^{2}\right)^{L}},

(244)

\displaystyle\frac{\log(N)}{N^{\frac{1}{D_{\mathcal{X}}+1}}}\leq\frac{0.25\eta^{2}}{Q_{1}+Q_{2}\log\left(\frac{2}{\rho}\right)},

(245)

\displaystyle L_{P}Q_{3}C_{\nabla w}\lVert\nabla\tau\rVert_{\infty}\left(\sum_{k=1}^{L}\binom{L}{k}\Delta_{\Theta}^{k}\right)+Q^{(T)}N_{P_{\tau}}\leq 0.5\eta,

(246)

\displaystyle C_{\nabla w}\lVert\nabla\tau\rVert_{\infty}\leq\frac{0.5\eta-Q^{(T)}N_{P_{\tau}}}{L_{P}\left(\sum_{k=1}^{L}\binom{L}{k}\Delta_{\Theta}^{k}\right)Q_{3}+U_{\tilde{C_{3}}^{(T)}}},

(247)

the DA generalization bound in Theorem
3.1
satisfies

\sqrt{\frac{C\Delta_{D}}{\lambda_{r}}}\left(L_{NN}\left(\sqrt{\Delta_{N}}+\Delta_{\Gamma,\Theta}\right)+G_{NN}\right)\leq\xi\epsilon_{S}(h,g_{D}),

(248)

and as a result
\epsilon_{T}(h,g_{D})\leq(1+\xi)\epsilon_{S}(h,g_{D})
.

Condition in Eq. (
244
) requires a limited perturbation strength over the MLP classifier.
Condition in Eq. (
246
) requires a limited perturbation strength over the MPNN feature extractor through both network weighs and RGM deformation.
Condition in Eq. (
245
) imposes a sample complexity requirement on graph size.
The last condition in Eq. (
247
) requires properties on RGMs.

Proof.

First, we seek to bound
\Delta_{D}
, originally defined in Lem.
E.2
.

The assumption on
M
and
D_{\mathcal{X}}
results in

M^{-\frac{1}{D_{\mathcal{X}}}}+M^{-\frac{1}{4}}\cdot\log(1/\rho)^{\frac{1}{4}}\leq\left(1+\log(1/\rho)^{\frac{1}{4}}\right)M^{-\frac{D_{\mathcal{X}}}{4}}\leq 0.1\times 27^{\frac{D_{\mathcal{X}}}{4}}

(249)

It then has

\displaystyle\sqrt{\Delta_{D}}\leq\;

\displaystyle\sqrt{2L_{2}}CL_{f}\bigg[\mathcal{W}_{2}\left(\hat{P}_{S}^{m},\hat{P}_{T}^{m}\right)+2B\cdot\left(1+\log(1/\rho)^{\frac{1}{4}}\right)M^{-\frac{D_{\mathcal{X}}}{4}}+2B\cdot 27^{\frac{D_{\mathcal{X}}}{4}}\bigg]^{\alpha}

\displaystyle\leq\;

\displaystyle\sqrt{2L_{2}}CL_{f}\bigg(\mathcal{W}_{2}\left(\hat{P}_{S}^{m},\hat{P}_{T}^{m}\right)+2B\cdot 1.1\times 27^{\frac{D_{\mathcal{X}}}{4}}\bigg)^{\alpha}.

(250)

Second, we seek to analyze
\Delta_{N}
, originally defined in Thm.
F.5
.

We approach to
L_{NN}
and
G_{NN}
, originally defined in Lem.
E.5
. According to definition, it has

\displaystyle L_{NN}\leq\;

\displaystyle L_{\sigma}^{L}\lambda_{M}^{L}=U_{L_{NN}},

(251)

\displaystyle G_{NN}\leq\;

\displaystyle\left(\frac{\beta}{\alpha}\kappa^{2}\right)^{L}\left((1+\Delta_{M})^{L}-1\right)=\left(\frac{\beta}{\alpha}\kappa^{2}\right)^{L}\left(\sum_{k=1}^{L}\binom{L}{k}\Delta_{M}^{k}\right)=U_{G_{NN}},

(252)

We now deal with
\{C_{i}^{(l)}\}_{i=1}^{2},\{D_{i}^{(l)}\}_{i=1}^{3}
, originally defined in Eqs. (
110
) - (
114
).

Under the assumption of formal bias, it has
C_{1}^{(l)},D_{1}^{(l)}=0
.
Again, according to definitions, it has

C_{2}^{(l)}=\prod_{t=1}^{l}L_{\Psi^{(t)}}\left(1+L_{\Phi^{(t)}}\right)\leq L_{P}^{l}(1+L_{P})^{l},

(253)

also

\displaystyle D_{2}^{(l)}=\;

\displaystyle\sum_{t=1}^{l}C_{2}^{(t-1)}\left(L_{\Psi^{(t)}}L_{\Phi^{(t)}}L_{W}^{\infty}d_{\min}^{-1}+L_{\Psi^{(t)}}L_{\Phi^{(t)}}\lVert W\rVert_{\infty}L_{W}^{\infty}d_{\min}^{-2}\right)

\displaystyle\cdot\prod_{l^{\prime}=t+1}^{l}L_{\Psi^{(l^{\prime})}}\left(1+\lVert W\rVert_{\infty}d_{\min}^{-1}L_{\Phi^{(l^{\prime})}}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\;

\displaystyle\sum_{t=1}^{l}L_{P}^{l+2}(1+L_{P})^{l}L_{W}^{\infty}d_{\min}^{-1}\left(1+\lVert W\rVert_{\infty}d_{\min}^{-1}\right)\cdot\left(L_{P}+\lVert W\rVert_{\infty}d_{\min}^{-1}L_{P}^{2}\right)^{l-t}

\displaystyle=\;

\displaystyle L_{P}^{2l+2}(1+L_{P})^{l}L_{W}^{\infty}d_{\min}^{-1}\left(1+\lVert W\rVert_{\infty}d_{\min}^{-1}\right)\left(1+\lVert W\rVert_{\infty}d_{\min}^{-1}L_{P}\right)^{l}

\displaystyle\cdot\sum_{t=1}^{l}\left(L_{P}+\lVert W\rVert_{\infty}d_{\min}^{-1}L_{P}^{2}\right)^{-t}

\displaystyle\leq\;

\displaystyle l\cdot L_{P}^{2l+2}(1+L_{P})^{l}L_{W}^{\infty}d_{\min}^{-1}\left(1+\lVert W\rVert_{\infty}d_{\min}^{-1}\right)\left(1+\lVert W\rVert_{\infty}d_{\min}^{-1}L_{P}\right)^{l}

\displaystyle\cdot\max\left(\left(L_{P}+\lVert W\rVert_{\infty}d_{\min}^{-1}L_{P}^{2}\right)^{-1},\left(L_{P}+\lVert W\rVert_{\infty}d_{\min}^{-1}L_{P}^{2}\right)^{-l}\right),

(254)

and

D_{3}^{(l)}=1+\lVert W\rVert_{\infty}d_{\min}^{-1}L_{\Phi^{(t)}}\leq 1+\lVert W\rVert_{\infty}d_{\min}^{-1}L_{P},

(255)

We now seek to bound
\{K_{i}\}_{i=1}^{9}
, originally defined in Eqs. (
F.4
).

Originally defined in Eq. (
133
), the constant
A^{(l)}
satisfies

A^{(l)}=L_{\Psi^{(l)}}\sqrt{1+\frac{8\lVert W\rVert_{\infty}^{2}L_{\Phi^{(l)}}^{2}}{d_{\min}^{2}}}\leq L_{P}\sqrt{1+8\lVert W\rVert_{\infty}^{2}L_{P}^{2}d_{\min}^{-2}},

(256)

thus

\prod_{l^{\prime}=l+1}^{T}A^{(l^{\prime})}\leq\left(L_{P}\sqrt{1+8\lVert W\rVert_{\infty}^{2}L_{P}^{2}d_{\min}^{-2}}\right)^{T-l}.

(257)

And it has

\displaystyle\sum_{l=1}^{T}C_{2}^{(l-1)}\prod_{l^{\prime}=l+1}^{T}A^{(l^{\prime})}\leq\;

\displaystyle\sum_{l=1}^{T}L_{P}^{l}(1+L_{P})^{l}\left(L_{P}\sqrt{1+8\lVert W\rVert_{\infty}^{2}L_{P}^{2}d_{\min}^{-2}}\right)^{T-l}

\displaystyle\leq\;

\displaystyle L_{P}^{T}\left(\sqrt{1+8\lVert W\rVert_{\infty}^{2}L_{P}^{2}d_{\min}^{-2}}\right)^{T}\sum_{l=1}^{T}(1+L_{P})^{l}\left(\sqrt{1+8\lVert W\rVert_{\infty}^{2}L_{P}^{2}d_{\min}^{-2}}\right)^{-l}

\displaystyle\leq\;

\displaystyle TL_{P}^{T}(1+L_{P})^{T}\left(\sqrt{1+8\lVert W\rVert_{\infty}^{2}L_{P}^{2}d_{\min}^{-2}}\right)^{T-1},

(258)

and

\displaystyle\sum_{l=1}^{T}D_{3}^{(l-1)}\prod_{l^{\prime}=l+1}^{T}A^{(l^{\prime})}\leq\;

\displaystyle\left(1+\lVert W\rVert_{\infty}d_{\min}^{-1}L_{P}\right)\sum_{l=1}^{T}\left(\sqrt{1+8\lVert W\rVert_{\infty}^{2}L_{P}^{2}d_{\min}^{-2}}\right)^{T-l}

\displaystyle=\;

\displaystyle T\left(1+\lVert W\rVert_{\infty}d_{\min}^{-1}L_{P}\right)\left(1+8\lVert W\rVert_{\infty}^{2}L_{P}^{2}d_{\min}^{-2}\right)^{\frac{T-1}{2}}

(259)

As a result, the quantities
\{K_{i}\}_{i=1}^{9}
satisfy

\displaystyle K_{1}=\;

\displaystyle 0,

\displaystyle K_{2}=\;

\displaystyle 0,

\displaystyle K_{3}\leq\;

\displaystyle 2TL_{P}^{T+2}(1+L_{P})^{T}\left(\sqrt{1+8\lVert W\rVert_{\infty}^{2}L_{P}^{2}d_{\min}^{-2}}\right)^{T-1}L_{W}^{\infty}\left(\sqrt{\log(C_{\mathcal{X}})}+\sqrt{D_{\mathcal{X}}}\right)\lVert W\rVert_{\infty}d_{\min}^{-2}

\displaystyle K_{4}\leq\;

\displaystyle\frac{2TL_{P}^{T+2}(1+L_{P})^{T}\left(\sqrt{2}\left\lVert W\right\rVert_{\infty}+L_{W}^{\infty}\right)\lVert W\rVert_{\infty}\left(\sqrt{1+8\lVert W\rVert_{\infty}^{2}L_{P}^{2}d_{\min}^{-2}}\right)^{T-1}d_{\min}^{-2}}{\sqrt{N}}

\displaystyle K_{5}=\;

\displaystyle 0,

\displaystyle K_{6}\leq\;

\displaystyle 2TL_{P}^{T+2}(1+L_{P})^{T}L_{W}^{\infty}\left(\sqrt{1+8\lVert W\rVert_{\infty}^{2}L_{P}^{2}d_{\min}^{-2}}\right)^{T-1}d_{\min}^{-1}=U_{K_{6}},

\displaystyle K_{7}\leq\;

\displaystyle 2TL_{P}^{2}\lVert W\rVert_{\infty}\left(1+\lVert W\rVert_{\infty}d_{\min}^{-1}L_{P}\right)d_{\min}^{-1}\left(1+8\lVert W\rVert_{\infty}^{2}L_{P}^{2}d_{\min}^{-2}\right)^{\frac{T-1}{2}}=U_{K_{7}},

\displaystyle K_{8}=\;

\displaystyle 0,

\displaystyle K_{9}<\;

\displaystyle 0.8TL_{P}^{T+2}(1+L_{P})^{T}C_{\mathcal{X}}\lVert W\rVert_{\infty}\left(\sqrt{1+8\lVert W\rVert_{\infty}^{2}L_{P}^{2}d_{\min}^{-2}}\right)^{T-1}d_{\min}^{-1}=U_{K_{9}}.

(260)

Based on the above, the following quantities satisfy

\displaystyle R_{1}=R_{2}=0,

\displaystyle R_{3}\leq 56T^{2}L_{P}^{2T+4}(1+L_{P})^{2T}\left(1+8\lVert W\rVert_{\infty}^{2}L_{P}^{2}d_{\min}^{-2}\right)^{T-1}(L_{W}^{\infty})^{2}\left(\sqrt{\log(C_{\mathcal{X}})}+\sqrt{D_{\mathcal{X}}}\right)^{2}\lVert W\rVert_{\infty}^{2}d_{\min}^{-4}=U_{R_{3}},

\displaystyle R_{4}\leq\frac{56TL_{P}^{2T+4}(1+L_{P})^{2T}\left(\sqrt{2}\left\lVert W\right\rVert_{\infty}+L_{W}^{\infty}\right)^{2}\lVert W\rVert_{\infty}^{2}\left(1+8\lVert W\rVert_{\infty}^{2}L_{P}^{2}d_{\min}^{-2}\right)^{T-1}d_{\min}^{-4}}{N}=\frac{U_{R_{4}}}{N},

\displaystyle S_{1}=S_{4}=0,

\displaystyle S_{2}\leq 14U_{K_{6}}^{2}+14U_{K_{9}}^{2}\log(C_{\mathcal{X}})+56\left(1+\lVert W\rVert_{\infty}d_{\min}^{-1}L_{P}\right)^{2}+7L_{P}^{2l}(1+L_{P})^{2l}C_{{\mathcal{X}}}^{2}\log(C_{\mathcal{X}})=U_{S_{2}},

\displaystyle S_{3}\leq 14U_{K_{7}}^{2}+56\left(1+\lVert W\rVert_{\infty}d_{\min}^{-1}L_{P}\right)^{2}L_{f}^{2}=U_{S_{3}},

\displaystyle S_{5}\leq 14U_{K_{9}}^{2}+7C_{\mathcal{X}}^{2}L_{P}^{2l}(1+L_{P})^{2l}=U_{S_{5}},

\displaystyle T_{1}=0,

\displaystyle T_{2}\leq\left(14U_{K_{9}}^{2}+7C_{\mathcal{X}}^{2}L_{P}^{2l}(1+L_{P})^{2l}\right)\frac{D_{\mathcal{X}}}{2(D_{\mathcal{X}}+1)}=U_{T_{2}},

(261)

where
\{R_{i}\}_{i=1}^{4},\{S_{i}\}_{i=1}^{5},\{T_{i}\}_{i=1}^{2}
are originally defined in Eqs. (
F.5
).

Assuming the graph size
N>2
and given that
\frac{1}{\log(2)}<1.5
and
\frac{1}{N}<\frac{log(N)}{N^{\frac{1}{D_{\mathcal{X}}+1}}}
, the above results in the following:

\displaystyle\Delta_{N}=\;

\displaystyle\frac{S_{2}\lVert f\rVert_{\infty}^{2}+S_{3}L_{f}^{2}+T_{2}\lVert f\rVert_{\infty}^{2}\log(N)}{N^{\frac{1}{D_{\mathcal{X}}+1}}}+\left(\frac{R_{3}+R_{4}\lVert f\rVert_{\infty}^{2}}{N}+\frac{S_{5}\lVert f\rVert_{\infty}^{2}}{N^{\frac{1}{D_{\mathcal{X}}+1}}}\right)\log\left(\frac{2}{\rho}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\;

\displaystyle\frac{\log(N)}{N^{\frac{1}{D_{\mathcal{X}}+1}}}\left[U_{T_{2}}\lVert f\rVert_{\infty}^{2}+\frac{1}{\log{N}}\left(U_{S_{2}}\lVert f\rVert_{\infty}^{2}+U_{S_{3}}L_{f}^{2}+\log\left(\frac{2}{\rho}\right)U_{S_{5}}\lVert f\rVert_{\infty}^{2}\right)+log\left(\frac{2}{\rho}\right)\left(U_{R_{3}}+\frac{U_{R_{4}}\lVert f\rVert_{\infty}^{2}}{N}\right)\right]

\displaystyle<\;

\displaystyle\frac{\log(N)}{N^{\frac{1}{D_{\mathcal{X}}+1}}}\left[U_{T_{2}}\lVert f\rVert_{\infty}^{2}+1.5\left(U_{S_{2}}\lVert f\rVert_{\infty}^{2}+U_{S_{3}}L_{f}^{2}+\log\left(\frac{2}{\rho}\right)U_{S_{5}}\lVert f\rVert_{\infty}^{2}\right)+log\left(\frac{2}{\rho}\right)\left(U_{R_{3}}+0.5U_{R_{4}}\lVert f\rVert_{\infty}^{2}\right)\right]

\displaystyle=\;

\displaystyle\frac{\log(N)}{N^{\frac{1}{D_{\mathcal{X}}+1}}}\left[U_{T_{2}}\lVert f\rVert_{\infty}^{2}+1.5\left(U_{S_{2}}\lVert f\rVert_{\infty}^{2}+U_{S_{3}}L_{f}^{2}\right)+log\left(\frac{2}{\rho}\right)\left(1.5U_{S_{5}}\lVert f\rVert_{\infty}^{2}+U_{R_{3}}+0.5U_{R_{4}}\lVert f\rVert_{\infty}^{2}\right)\right].

(262)

Defining new quantities
Q_{1}=U_{T_{2}}\lVert f\rVert_{\infty}^{2}+1.5\left(U_{S_{2}}\lVert f\rVert_{\infty}^{2}+U_{S_{3}}L_{f}^{2}\right)
and

Q_{2}=1.5U_{S_{5}}\lVert f\rVert_{\infty}^{2}+U_{R_{3}}+0.5U_{R_{4}}\lVert f\rVert_{\infty}^{2}

, it then has

\Delta_{N}\leq\frac{\log(N)}{N^{\frac{1}{D_{\mathcal{X}}+1}}}\left(Q_{1}+Q_{2}\log\left(\frac{2}{\rho}\right)\right).

(263)

Third, we analyze optimization error bound
\Delta_{\Gamma,\Theta}
, originally defined in Thm.
G.5
.

We further simplify the following quantities:

\displaystyle C_{3}^{(T)}=\;

\displaystyle\frac{d_{\min}+W_{\max}}{d_{\min}^{2}}\sum_{l=1}^{T}L_{\Psi^{(l)}}L_{\Phi^{(l)}}C_{2}^{(l-1)}\lVert f\rVert_{\infty}\prod_{l^{\prime}=l+1}^{T}L_{\Psi^{(l^{\prime})}}\left(1+\frac{W_{\max}}{d_{\min}}\cdot L_{\Phi^{(l^{\prime})}}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\;

\displaystyle\frac{d_{\min}+W_{\max}}{d_{\min}^{2}}L_{P}^{T+1}\lVert f\rVert_{\infty}\left(1+\frac{W_{\max}}{d_{\min}}L_{P}\right)^{T}\sum_{l=1}^{T}(1+L_{P})^{l-1}\left(1+\frac{W_{\max}}{d_{\min}}L_{P}\right)^{-l}

\displaystyle<\;

\displaystyle\frac{(d_{\min}+W_{\max})\lVert f\rVert_{\infty}TL_{P}^{T+1}(1+L_{P})^{T-1}}{d_{\min}^{2}}\left(1+\frac{W_{\max}}{d_{\min}}L_{P}\right)^{T-1}

(264)

and

\displaystyle C_{4}^{(T)}=\;

\displaystyle\prod_{l=1}^{T}L_{\Psi^{(l)}}\left(1+\frac{W_{\max}}{d_{\min}}\cdot L_{\Phi^{(l)}}\right)\leq L_{P}^{T}\left(1+\frac{W_{\max}}{d_{\min}}L_{P}\right)^{T},

(265)

\displaystyle C_{5}^{(T)}=\;

\displaystyle C_{2}^{(T)}\lVert f\rVert_{\infty}\leq L_{P}^{T}(1+L_{P})^{T}\lVert f\rVert_{\infty}.

(266)

Then the following quantities, originally defined in Thm.
G.4
, satisfy

\displaystyle\Delta_{\Phi}^{(l+1)}=\sqrt{H_{l+1}}\left(\frac{\beta\kappa^{2}}{\alpha}\right)^{L}\left[\prod_{k=1}^{L}\left(1+\frac{\left\lVert\Delta\Theta_{\Phi_{k}^{(l+1)}}\right\rVert_{F}}{\left\lVert\Theta_{\Phi_{k}^{(l+1)}}\right\rVert_{F}}\right)-1\right]\leq\sqrt{H_{l+1}}\left(\frac{\beta\kappa^{2}}{\alpha}\right)^{L}\left(\left(1+\Delta_{\Theta}\right)^{L}-1\right),

(267)

\displaystyle\Delta_{\Psi}^{(l+1)}=\sqrt{F_{l+1}}\left(\frac{\beta\kappa^{2}}{\alpha}\right)^{L}\left[\prod_{k=1}^{L}\left(1+\frac{\lVert\Delta\Theta_{\Psi_{k}^{(l+1)}}\rVert_{F}}{\lVert\Theta_{\Psi_{k}^{(l+1)}}\rVert_{F}}\right)-1\right]\leq\sqrt{F_{l+1}}\left(\frac{\beta\kappa^{2}}{\alpha}\right)^{L}\left(\left(1+\Delta_{\Theta}\right)^{L}-1\right),

(268)

\displaystyle L_{\tilde{\Psi}^{(l)}}=L_{\sigma}^{L}\prod_{t=1}^{L}\left(\left\lVert\Delta\Theta_{\Psi_{t}^{(l)}}\right\rVert_{F}+\left\lVert\Theta_{\Psi_{t}^{(l)}}\right\rVert_{F}\right)\leq L_{\sigma}^{L}D_{\Theta}^{L}\left(1+\Delta_{\Theta}\right)^{L},

(269)

\displaystyle L_{\tilde{\Phi}^{(l)}}=L_{\sigma}^{L}\prod_{t=1}^{L}\left(\left\lVert\Delta\Theta_{\Phi_{t}^{(l)}}\right\rVert_{F}+\left\lVert\Theta_{\Phi_{t}^{(l)}}\right\rVert_{F}\right)\leq L_{\sigma}^{L}D_{\Theta}^{L}\left(1+\Delta_{\Theta}\right)^{L},

(270)

\displaystyle D_{\tilde{\Psi}^{(t)}(0,0)}=\left(\Delta_{\Psi}^{(t)}+1\right)\left\lVert\Psi^{(t)}(0,0)\right\rVert_{\infty}=0,

(271)

\displaystyle D_{\tilde{\Phi}^{(t)}(0,0)}=\left(\Delta_{\Phi}^{(t)}+1\right)\left\lVert\Phi^{(t)}(0,0)\right\rVert_{\infty}=0,

(272)

and the last two quantities result in
\left\lVert\tilde{\Phi}^{(t)}(0,0)\right\rVert_{\infty},\left\lVert\tilde{\Psi}^{(t)}(0,0)\right\rVert_{\infty}=0
.
Based on these, the following quantities satisfy

\displaystyle\tilde{C_{1}}^{(l)}=0,

(273)

\displaystyle\tilde{C_{2}}^{(l)}=\prod_{t=1}^{l}L_{\tilde{\Psi}^{(t)}}\left(1+L_{\tilde{\Phi}^{(t)}}\right)\leq\left(L_{\sigma}^{L}D_{\Theta}^{L}\left(1+\Delta_{\Theta}\right)^{L}\right)^{l}\left(1+L_{\sigma}^{L}D_{\Theta}^{L}\left(1+\Delta_{\Theta}\right)^{L}\right)^{l},

(274)

\displaystyle C_{\Phi}^{(l+1)}=L_{\Phi^{(l+1)}}C_{2}^{(l)}\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{\infty}\leq L_{P}^{l+1}(1+L_{P})^{l}\lVert f\rVert_{\infty},

(275)

\displaystyle\tilde{C}_{\Phi}^{(l+1)}\leq L_{P}\left(L_{\sigma}^{L}D_{\Theta}^{L}\left(1+\Delta_{\Theta}\right)^{L}\right)^{l}\left(1+L_{\sigma}^{L}D_{\Theta}^{L}\left(1+\Delta_{\Theta}\right)^{L}\right)^{l}\lVert f\rVert_{\infty}.

(276)

and the quantity, originally defined in Thm.
G.5
, satisfy

\displaystyle\tilde{C_{3}}^{(T)}=\;

\displaystyle\bigg(\frac{1}{d_{\min}}+\frac{W_{\max}}{d_{\min}^{2}}\bigg)\sum_{l=1}^{T}L_{\tilde{\Psi}^{(l)}}L_{\tilde{\Phi}^{(l)}}\tilde{C_{2}}^{(l-1)}\lVert f\rVert_{\infty}

\displaystyle\leq\;

\displaystyle\bigg(\frac{1}{d_{\min}}+\frac{W_{\max}}{d_{\min}^{2}}\bigg)\left(1+L_{\sigma}^{L}D_{\Theta}^{L}\left(1+\Delta_{\Theta}\right)^{L}\right)^{2}\lVert f\rVert_{\infty}\sum_{l=1}^{T}\tilde{C_{2}}^{(l-1)}

\displaystyle<\;

\displaystyle\bigg(\frac{1}{d_{\min}}+\frac{W_{\max}}{d_{\min}^{2}}\bigg)\left(1+L_{\sigma}^{L}D_{\Theta}^{L}\left(1+\Delta_{\Theta}\right)^{L}\right)^{T+2}\lVert f\rVert_{\infty}\sum_{l=1}^{T}\left(L_{\sigma}^{L}D_{\Theta}^{L}\left(1+\Delta_{\Theta}\right)^{L}\right)^{l}

\displaystyle\leq\;

\displaystyle T\bigg(\frac{1}{d_{\min}}+\frac{W_{\max}}{d_{\min}^{2}}\bigg)\left(1+L_{\sigma}^{L}D_{\Theta}^{L}\left(1+\Delta_{\Theta}\right)^{L}\right)^{T+2}\lVert f\rVert_{\infty}\cdot

\displaystyle\max\left(L_{\sigma}^{L}D_{\Theta}^{L}\left(1+\Delta_{\Theta}\right)^{L},\left(L_{\sigma}^{L}D_{\Theta}^{L}\left(1+\Delta_{\Theta}\right)^{L}\right)^{T}\right)=U_{\tilde{C_{3}}^{(T)}}.

(277)

Then, the following can be derived:

\displaystyle C_{T_{2}}^{(l+1)}=\;

\displaystyle L_{\Psi^{(l+1)}}\Delta_{\Phi}^{(l+1)}C_{\Phi}^{(l+1)}\frac{\lVert W\rVert_{\infty}}{d_{\min}}

\displaystyle\leq\;

\displaystyle\sqrt{H_{l+1}}\left(\frac{\beta\kappa^{2}}{\alpha}\right)^{L}L_{P}\left(\left(1+\Delta_{\Theta}\right)^{L}-1\right)L_{P}^{l+1}(1+L_{P})^{l}\frac{\lVert W\rVert_{\infty}\lVert f\rVert_{\infty}}{d_{\min}}

\displaystyle=\;

\displaystyle\sqrt{H_{l+1}}\left(\frac{\beta\kappa^{2}}{\alpha}\right)^{L}L_{P}^{l+2}(1+L_{P})^{l}\frac{\lVert W\rVert_{\infty}\lVert f\rVert_{\infty}}{d_{\min}}\left(\sum_{k=1}^{L}\binom{L}{k}\Delta_{\Theta}^{k}\right)=U_{C_{T_{2}}^{(l+1)}}\left(\sum_{k=1}^{L}\binom{L}{k}\Delta_{\Theta}^{k}\right),

(278)

\displaystyle C_{T_{3}}^{(l+1)}=\;

\displaystyle L_{\Psi^{(l+1)}}L_{\tilde{\Phi}^{(l)}}\frac{\lVert W\rVert_{\infty}}{d_{\min}}\leq L_{P}L_{\sigma}^{L}D_{\Theta}^{L}\left(1+\Delta_{\Theta}\right)^{L}\frac{\lVert W\rVert_{\infty}}{d_{\min}}=U_{C_{T_{3}}^{(l+1)}},

(279)

and

\displaystyle\tilde{C_{5}}^{(l+1)}=\;

\displaystyle\max\left(\tilde{C_{2}}^{(l+1)}\lVert f\rVert_{\infty},\frac{\left\lVert W\right\rVert_{\infty}}{d_{\min}}\left(\Delta_{\Phi}^{(l+1)}+1\right)\tilde{C}_{\Phi}^{(l+1)}\right),

\displaystyle\leq\;

\displaystyle\max\left(\left(L_{\sigma}^{L}D_{\Theta}^{L}\left(1+\Delta_{\Theta}\right)^{L}\right)^{l}\left(1+L_{\sigma}^{L}D_{\Theta}^{L}\left(1+\Delta_{\Theta}\right)^{L}\right)^{l}\lVert f\rVert_{\infty},\right.

\displaystyle\frac{\left\lVert W\right\rVert_{\infty}}{d_{\min}}\left(\sqrt{H_{l+1}}\left(\frac{\beta\kappa^{2}}{\alpha}\right)^{L}\left(\left(1+\Delta_{\Theta}\right)^{L}-1\right)+1\right)\times

\displaystyle\left.L_{P}\left(L_{\sigma}^{L}D_{\Theta}^{L}\left(1+\Delta_{\Theta}\right)^{L}\right)^{l}\left(1+L_{\sigma}^{L}D_{\Theta}^{L}\left(1+\Delta_{\Theta}\right)^{L}\right)^{l}\lVert f\rVert_{\infty}\right)=U_{C_{5}^{(l+1)}},

(280)

\displaystyle C_{T_{4}}^{(l+1)}=\;

\displaystyle\Delta_{\Psi}^{(l+1)}L_{\Psi^{(l+1)}}\tilde{C_{5}}^{(l)}\leq\sqrt{F_{l+1}}\left(\frac{\beta\kappa^{2}}{\alpha}\right)^{L}\left(\left(1+\Delta_{\Theta}\right)^{L}-1\right)L_{P}\tilde{C_{5}}^{(l)}

\displaystyle=\;

\displaystyle\sqrt{F_{l+1}}\left(\frac{\beta\kappa^{2}}{\alpha}\right)^{L}L_{P}U_{C_{5}^{(l+1)}}\left(\sum_{k=1}^{L}\binom{L}{k}\Delta_{\Theta}^{k}\right)=U_{C_{T_{4}}^{(l+1)}}\left(\sum_{k=1}^{L}\binom{L}{k}\Delta_{\Theta}^{k}\right).

(281)

The above inequalities result in

\displaystyle\Delta_{\Gamma,\Theta}=\;

\displaystyle\sum_{l=1}^{T}\left(C_{T_{2}}^{(l)}+C_{T_{4}}^{(l)}\right)\prod_{l^{\prime}=l+1}^{T}L_{\Psi^{(l^{\prime})}}\left(1+C_{T_{3}}^{(l^{\prime})}\right)+\tilde{C_{3}}^{(T)}C_{\nabla w}\lVert\nabla\tau\rVert_{\infty}+\tilde{C_{2}}^{(T)}\lVert f\rVert_{\infty}N_{P_{\tau}}

\displaystyle\leq\;

\displaystyle\sum_{l=1}^{T}\left(U_{C_{T_{2}}^{(l)}}+U_{C_{T_{4}}^{(l)}}\right)\left(\sum_{k=1}^{L}\binom{L}{k}\Delta_{\Theta}^{k}\right)L_{P}\prod_{l^{\prime}=l+1}^{T}\left(1+U_{C_{T_{3}}^{(l^{\prime})}}\right)+U_{\tilde{C_{3}}^{(T)}}C_{\nabla w}\lVert\nabla\tau\rVert_{\infty}+

\displaystyle\left(L_{\sigma}^{L}D_{\Theta}^{L}\left(1+\Delta_{\Theta}\right)^{L}\right)^{T}\left(1+L_{\sigma}^{L}D_{\Theta}^{L}\left(1+\Delta_{\Theta}\right)^{L}\right)^{T}\lVert f\rVert_{\infty}N_{P_{\tau}}

\displaystyle=\;

\displaystyle L_{P}\left(\sum_{k=1}^{L}\binom{L}{k}\Delta_{\Theta}^{k}\right)\sum_{l=1}^{T}\left(U_{C_{T_{2}}^{(l)}}+U_{C_{T_{4}}^{(l)}}\right)\prod_{l^{\prime}=l+1}^{T}\left(1+U_{C_{T_{3}}^{(l^{\prime})}}\right)C_{\nabla w}\lVert\nabla\tau\rVert_{\infty}+

\displaystyle U_{\tilde{C_{3}}^{(T)}}C_{\nabla w}\lVert\nabla\tau\rVert_{\infty}+\left(L_{\sigma}^{L}D_{\Theta}^{L}\left(1+\Delta_{\Theta}\right)^{L}\right)^{T}\left(1+L_{\sigma}^{L}D_{\Theta}^{L}\left(1+\Delta_{\Theta}\right)^{L}\right)^{T}\lVert f\rVert_{\infty}N_{P_{\tau}}.

(282)

Defining the following two new quantities,

\displaystyle Q^{(l)}=\left(L_{\sigma}^{L}D_{\Theta}^{L}\left(1+\Delta_{\Theta}\right)^{L}\right)^{l}\left(1+L_{\sigma}^{L}D_{\Theta}^{L}\left(1+\Delta_{\Theta}\right)^{L}\right)^{l}\lVert f\rVert_{\infty},

(283)

\displaystyle Q_{3}=\sum_{l=1}^{T}\left(U_{C_{T_{2}}^{(l)}}+U_{C_{T_{4}}^{(l)}}\right)\prod_{l^{\prime}=l+1}^{T}\left(1+U_{C_{T_{3}}^{(l^{\prime})}}\right),

(284)

it then has

\displaystyle U_{C_{5}^{(l+1)}}=Q^{(l)}\max\left(1,\frac{\left\lVert W\right\rVert_{\infty}}{d_{\min}}\left(\sqrt{H_{l+1}}\left(\frac{\beta\kappa^{2}}{\alpha}\right)^{L}\left(\sum_{k=1}^{L}\binom{L}{k}\Delta_{\Theta}^{k}\right)+1\right)L_{P}\right),

(285)

\displaystyle\Delta_{\Gamma,\Theta}\leq L_{P}\left(\sum_{k=1}^{L}\binom{L}{k}\Delta_{\Theta}^{k}\right)Q_{3}C_{\nabla w}\lVert\nabla\tau\rVert_{\infty}+U_{\tilde{C_{3}}^{(T)}}C_{\nabla w}\lVert\nabla\tau\rVert_{\infty}+Q^{(T)}N_{P_{\tau}}.

(286)

Finally, we are now ready to develop sufficient conditions for the bound

\sqrt{\frac{C\Delta_{D}}{\lambda_{r}}}\left(L_{NN}\left(\sqrt{\Delta_{N}}+\Delta_{\Gamma,\Theta}\right)+G_{NN}\right)

(287)

to be no more than
\xi\epsilon_{S}(h,g_{D})
with
\xi>0
, where the smaller
\xi
is, the tighter the bound becomes.
Applying Eqs. (
240
)-(
242
), we study sufficient conditions for the last inequality to hold

\displaystyle\sqrt{\frac{C\Delta_{D}}{\lambda_{r}}}\left(L_{NN}\left(\sqrt{\Delta_{N}}+\Delta_{\Gamma,\Theta}+\right)+G_{NN}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\;

\displaystyle\sqrt{\frac{C}{\lambda_{r}}}U_{\sqrt{\Delta_{D}}}\left(U_{L_{NN}}\left(\sqrt{U_{\Delta_{N}}}+U_{\Delta_{\Gamma,\Theta}}\right)+U_{G_{NN}}\right)\leq\xi\epsilon_{S}(h,g_{D}).

(288)

It requires

U_{L_{NN}}\left(\sqrt{U_{\Delta_{N}}}+U_{\Delta_{\Gamma,\Theta}}\right)+U_{G_{NN}}\leq\frac{\xi\epsilon_{S}(h,g_{D})\sqrt{\lambda_{r}}}{\sqrt{2L_{2}}C^{1.5}L_{f}\bigg(\mathcal{W}_{2}\left(\hat{P}_{S}^{m},\hat{P}_{T}^{m}\right)+2B\cdot 1.1\times 27^{\frac{D_{\mathcal{X}}}{4}}\bigg)^{\alpha}},

(289)

which in turn requires the following:

\displaystyle U_{G_{NN}}\leq\frac{\xi\epsilon_{S}(h,g_{D})\sqrt{\lambda_{r}}}{\sqrt{2L_{2}}C^{1.5}L_{f}\bigg(\mathcal{W}_{2}\left(\hat{P}_{S}^{m},\hat{P}_{T}^{m}\right)+2B\cdot 1.1\times 27^{\frac{D_{\mathcal{X}}}{4}}\bigg)^{\alpha}},

(290)

\displaystyle\sqrt{U_{\Delta_{N}}}+U_{\Delta_{\Gamma,\Theta}}\leq\frac{\frac{\xi\epsilon_{S}(h,g_{D})\sqrt{\lambda_{r}}}{\sqrt{2L_{2}}C^{1.5}L_{f}\bigg(\mathcal{W}_{2}\left(\hat{P}_{S}^{m},\hat{P}_{T}^{m}\right)+2B\cdot 1.1\times 27^{\frac{D_{\mathcal{X}}}{4}}\bigg)^{\alpha}}-U_{G_{NN}}}{U_{L_{NN}}}=\eta.

(291)

Applying the expression of
U_{G_{NN}}
, the first condition as in Eq. (
290
) results in Eq. (
244
), requiring a limited perturbation strength over the MLP classifier, as

\sum_{k=1}^{L}\binom{L}{k}\Delta_{M}^{k}\leq\frac{\xi\epsilon_{S}(h,g_{D})\sqrt{\lambda_{r}}}{\sqrt{2L_{2}}C^{1.5}L_{f}\bigg(\mathcal{W}_{2}\left(\hat{P}_{S}^{m},\hat{P}_{T}^{m}\right)+2B\cdot 1.1\times 27^{\frac{D_{\mathcal{X}}}{4}}\bigg)^{\alpha}\left(\frac{\beta}{\alpha}\kappa^{2}\right)^{L}}.

(292)

For the sake of convenience, we develop conditions for
\sqrt{U_{\Delta_{N}}}\leq 0.5\eta
and
U_{\Delta_{\Gamma,\Theta}}\leq 0.5\eta
, separately.
Applying Eq. (
241
), it requires

\frac{\log(N)}{N^{\frac{1}{D_{\mathcal{X}}+1}}}\leq\frac{0.25\eta^{2}}{Q_{1}+Q_{2}\log\left(\frac{2}{\rho}\right)}.

(293)

Applying Eq. (
242
), it requires

L_{P}\left(\sum_{k=1}^{L}\binom{L}{k}\Delta_{\Theta}^{k}\right)Q_{3}C_{\nabla w}\lVert\nabla\tau\rVert_{\infty}+U_{\tilde{C_{3}}^{(T)}}C_{\nabla w}\lVert\nabla\tau\rVert_{\infty}+Q^{(T)}N_{P_{\tau}}\leq 0.5\eta.

(294)

This in turn requires a limited perturbation strength over the MPNN feature extractor through both network weighs and RGM deformation, given as

\left[L_{P}\left(\sum_{k=1}^{L}\binom{L}{k}\Delta_{\Theta}^{k}\right)Q_{3}+U_{\tilde{C_{3}}^{(T)}}\right]C_{\nabla w}\lVert\nabla\tau\rVert_{\infty}+Q^{(T)}N_{P_{\tau}}\leq 0.5\eta,

(295)

and conditions over the RGMs, as

C_{\nabla w}\lVert\nabla\tau\rVert_{\infty}\leq\frac{0.5\eta-Q^{(T)}N_{P_{\tau}}}{L_{P}\left(\sum_{k=1}^{L}\binom{L}{k}\Delta_{\Theta}^{k}\right)Q_{3}+U_{\tilde{C_{3}}^{(T)}}}.

(296)

These correspond to the four conditions in Eqs. (
244
)-(
247
), which together result in Eq. (
248
).

∎

Appendix J
Third-Party Results

To be self-contained, we re-state in this section the existing results that support our result development.

Lemma J.1

(Lemma 4,
59
)
.

Consider an RGM
\Gamma=(W,P,f)
satisfying Ass.
C.1
and
C.2
, we draw a graph
G_{N}\sim\Gamma
with
N
nodes
x_{1},\cdots,x_{N}
. Let
\rho\in(0,1)
. Then, with probability at least
1-\rho
, the following holds:

\displaystyle\left\lVert\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}W(\cdot,x_{i})f(x_{i})-\int W(\cdot,x)f(x)dP(x)\right\rVert_{\infty}

\displaystyle\lesssim\;

\displaystyle\frac{\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{\infty}\left(L_{W}^{\infty}(\sqrt{\log(C_{\mathcal{X}})}+\sqrt{D_{\mathcal{X}}})+(\sqrt{2}\left\lVert W\right\rVert_{\infty}+L_{W})\sqrt{\log\left(\frac{2}{\rho}\right)}\right)}{\sqrt{N}}.

(297)

Remark J.2
.

Re-written by assuming
\mathcal{N}(\mathcal{X},\epsilon,d)\leq C_{\mathcal{X}}\cdot\epsilon^{-D_{\mathcal{X}}}
and ignoring constants. Original results obtained using Hoeffding’s inequality, Dudley’s inequality (Thm 8.1.6,
102
), and other results in (Sec 2,
102
).

Lemma J.3

(Lemma 3,
77
)
.

Given a compact metric space
(\mathcal{X},d)
satisfying Ass.
C.1
and a
L_{F}
-Lipschitz continuous function
f:\mathcal{X}\to\mathbb{R}^{F}
. Suppose a set of points
X_{N}=\{x_{i}\}_{i=1}^{N}
are sampled from probability distribution
P
in space
\mathcal{X}
. Then, the following holds with probability at least
1-\rho
:

\displaystyle\left\lVert\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}F(x_{i})-\int_{\mathcal{X}}F(x)\,dP(x)\right\rVert_{\infty}

\displaystyle\leq\;

\displaystyle N^{-\frac{1}{2(D_{\mathcal{X}}+1)}}\left(2L_{F}+\frac{C_{\mathcal{X}}\lVert F\rVert_{\infty}}{\sqrt{2}}\sqrt{\log(C_{\mathcal{X}})+\frac{D_{\mathcal{X}}\log(N)}{2(D_{\mathcal{X}+1})}+\log\left(\frac{2}{\rho}\right)}\right).

(298)

Remark J.4
.

Obtained by exploiting covering balls structures in space
\mathcal{X}
. Hoeffding’s inequality is used to bound the error of Monte Carlo approximation towards a single covering ball, i.e. points in
X_{N}
that fall into a single ball can be regarded as an approximation of this ball which leads to an approximation error. A joint event, where such small approximation errors hold for all balls, is constructed with respect to choice of samples
X_{N}=\{x_{i}\}_{i=1}^{N}
. As indicated in the lemma, such event occurs with probability at least
1-\rho
.

Theorem J.5

(Theorem 7,
59
)
.

Let
(\mathcal{X},d)
be a compact metric space with
diam(\mathcal{X})\leq B
and
\mathcal{N}(\mathcal{X},\epsilon,d)\leq(B/\epsilon)^{D_{\mathcal{X}}}
. Let
P
be a probability measure in
\mathcal{X}
and
x_{1},\cdots,x_{n}
drawn i.i.d. from
P
and define
\hat{P}=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i}\delta_{x_{i}}
as the empirical probability measure through Monte Carlo sampling. Let
\rho\in(0,1)
. Then, with probability
1-\rho
the following holds:

\mathcal{W}_{2}\left(P,\hat{P}\right)\lesssim B\left(n^{-\frac{1}{D_{\mathcal{X}}}}+\left(27^{\frac{D_{\mathcal{X}}}{4}}+\log(1/\rho)^{\frac{1}{4}}n^{-\frac{1}{4}}\right)\right)
.

Remark J.6
.

Obtained by combining Prop. 5 and Prop. 20 in
107
with
\epsilon^{\prime}=1
, applicable to any cost function used for defining Wasserstein distance.

Theorem J.7

(Theorem 1,
14
)
.

Let
f
be two multi-layer perceptrons with nonlinearity
\varphi
and
L
weight matrices
\{W_{l}\}_{l=1}^{L}
. Define
\tilde{f}
the multi-layer perceptron with the same architecture but different weight matrices
\{\tilde{W}_{l}\}_{l=1}^{L}
. Specifically, let
\{\Delta W_{l}=\tilde{W}_{l}-W_{l}\}_{l=1}^{L}
be the layer-wise perturbation. If there exists
\alpha,\beta>0
s.t.
\forall x,y
:

\alpha\lVert x\rVert\leq\lVert\varphi(x)\rVert\leq\beta\lVert x\rVert,

(299)

\alpha\lVert x-y\rVert\leq\lVert\varphi(x)-\varphi(y)\rVert\leq\beta\lVert x-y\rVert,

(300)

and all matrices
\{W_{l}\}_{l=1}^{L}
,
\{\tilde{W}_{l}\}_{l=1}^{L}
, and perturbations
\{\Delta W_{l}\}_{l=1}^{L}
have condition number, i.e. ratio of largest to smallest singular value, no larger than
\kappa
. Then, for all
x\in\mathbb{R}^{D}
, the following holds:

\frac{\lVert\tilde{f}(x)-f(x)\rVert}{\lVert f(x)\rVert}\leq\left(\frac{\beta}{\alpha}\kappa^{2}\right)^{L}\left[\prod_{k=1}^{L}\left(1+\frac{\lVert\Delta W_{k}\rVert_{F}}{\lVert W_{k}\rVert_{F}}\right)-1\right].

(301)

Appendix K
Exp 1: Latent Wasserstein Distance as Domain Shift Indicator

In this section, we present experimental details and results to assess latent Wasserstein distance as domain shift indicator.

Data and protocol.

For data, we use the four sub-datasets of predictive toxicology challenge (PTC)
[
46
,
82
]
as domains:

D=\{\texttt{PTC\_FM},\texttt{PTC\_MM},\texttt{PTC\_FR},\texttt{PTC\_MR}\}
.
For each source domain
S\in D
, we train a GIN classifier on
S
(train ratio
0.8
) and evaluate on all target domains
T\in D
, producing a
4\times 4
test-loss matrix
\mathbf{L}
, where
L_{S,T}
is the mean test loss from source
S
to target
T
.
All classifier results are averaged over seeds
\{0,1,2\}
.

Subgroup

#  Graphs

mean nodes

Table 4:
Summary statistics of the four PTC subgroups.

Latent position estimation.

We adopt the latent distance model of
48
.
For each graph
G=(V,E)
with binary adjacency
Y\in\{0,1\}^{|V|\times|V|}
and dyad covariates
x_{ij}\in\mathbb{R}^{p}
, where
x_{ij}
is a feature vector constructed from node attributes of the pair
(i,j)
.
In our implementation, we use the concatenation
x_{ij}=\textmd{concat}[|x_{i}-x_{j}|,\,(x_{i}-x_{j})^{\odot 2}]
) where
\odot 2
indicates element-wise square operation.
the model assigns each node
i\in V
a latent position
z_{i}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
and assumes conditional dyadic independence:

P(Y\mid Z,\alpha,\beta)=\prod_{i<j}\mathrm{Bern}\!\left(Y_{ij};\,p_{ij}\right),\qquad\mathrm{logit}(p_{ij})=\alpha+\beta^{\top}x_{ij}-\|z_{i}-z_{j}\|_{2},

where
Z=\{z_{i}\}_{i\in V}
,
\alpha\in\mathbb{R}
is an intercept and
\beta\in\mathbb{R}^{p}
are covariate coefficients.
This parameterization makes tie probabilities decrease monotonically with latent distance.
The likelihood is invariant to global translation and orthogonal transforms of
Z
, so
Z
is identifiable only up to such transformations.
For each domain
S\in D
and class
c\in\{0,1\}
, we estimate
(\alpha,\beta)
and per-graph latent coordinates
Z
by maximum likelihood,
followed by post-processing (alignment/pooling) to obtain graph-level representations.

Graph-level representation and Wasserstein distance.

For each graph
G
, let
\hat{Z}_{G}=\{\hat{z}_{i}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\}_{i\in V}
denote the post-processed latent positions, after Procrustes alignment, and after size-controlled subsampling (
n=16
for each graph).
We then map the latent point cloud to a graph-level vector

\phi(G)=\Big[\mu(\hat{Z}_{G}),\,\mathrm{Var}(\hat{Z}_{G})\Big]\in\mathbb{R}^{2d},

i.e., concatenated per-dimension mean and variance (
mean_var
,
2d=4
).
For each domain
S\in D
and class
c\in\{0,1\}
, this yields graph-level samples

\Phi_{S,c}=\{\phi(G):G\in\mathcal{D}_{S,c}\},

where
\mathcal{D}_{S,c}
is the set of graphs in domain
S
with label
c
.
We compute the class-wise entropic
2
-Wasserstein (Sinkhorn) distance

W_{S,T}^{(c)}\;=\;W_{2,\varepsilon}\!\left(\Phi_{S,c},\Phi_{T,c}\right),

with
\varepsilon=0.1
, 1000 Sinkhorn iterations, and a per-class sample cap of 2000, then aggregate

W_{S,T}=\sum_{c\in\{0,1\}}W_{S,T}^{(c)}.

The resulting WD matrix
\mathbf{W}
is averaged over random seeds
\{0,1,2\}
.

Figure 3
:
Estimated latent WD correlates with test losses.

Evaluation and Results.

We evaluate whether the estimated shift tracks performance degradation by correlating
\{W_{S,T}\}_{S\neq T}
with
\{L_{S,T}\}_{S\neq T}
over the 12 off-diagonal directed domain pairs.
We report Pearson correlation coefficient (PCC) and Spearman rank correlation (SRC).
As shown in Figure
3
, the off-diagonal graph-level latent Wasserstein values are in a narrow but structured range (
\approx 0.005
–
0.009
), while the test-loss matrix shows clear cross-domain degradation patterns.
The correlation between
\mathbf{W}
and
\mathbf{L}
is:

\mathrm{PCC}=0.751\;(p=0.00488),\qquad\mathrm{SRC}=0.580\;(p=0.0479).

Both are significantly positive at conventional levels, indicating that graph-level latent WD is aligned with cross-domain test-loss degradation in this PTC setting.

Takeaway.

Using graph-level latent summaries together with Procrustes alignment yields a statistically significant association between estimated shift and downstream degradation.

Appendix L
Exp 2: Spectrum Geometry

In this section, we introduce our experiment details and how we estimate empirical spectrum of kernel integral operator.

L.1
Empirical Spectrum Estimation

Let
k:\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{X}\to\mathbb{R}
be a positive semidefinite kernel and let
\mu
be a probability measure on
\mathcal{X}
.
The associated kernel integral operator
T_{k}:L_{2}(\mu)\to L_{2}(\mu)
is defined by

(T_{k}f)(x)\;:=\;\int_{\mathcal{X}}k(x,x^{\prime})\,f(x^{\prime})\,d\mu(x^{\prime}).

(302)

Given i.i.d. samples
\{x_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n}\sim\mu
, we form the empirical measure
\mu_{n}:=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\delta_{x_{i}}
and the empirical operator

(T_{k,n}f)(x)\;:=\;\int_{\mathcal{X}}k(x,x^{\prime})\,f(x^{\prime})\,d\mu_{n}(x^{\prime})\;=\;\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}k(x,x_{i})\,f(x_{i}).

(303)

Restricting the eigen-equation
T_{k,n}\phi=\lambda\phi
to the sample points yields

\frac{1}{n}Kv\;=\;\lambda v,\qquad K_{ij}:=k(x_{i},x_{j}),\;\;v_{i}:=\phi(x_{i}),

(304)

so the nonzero eigenvalues of
T_{k,n}
coincide with those of
\frac{1}{n}K
.
Therefore, eigen-decomposition of the normalized Gram matrix provides a finite-sample spectral estimate of
T_{k}
.

The results of this estimation depend on:

•

sample size

n
, which controls how well
\mu_{n}
approximates
\mu
and hence how close
T_{k,n}
is to
T_{k}
;

•

kernel choice and regularity
, which determine whether
T_{k}
is compact and how rapidly its spectrum decays;

•

rank constraints & scaling effects
when
k(x,x^{\prime})=\langle\phi(x),\phi(x^{\prime})\rangle
is induced by learned embeddings, since
K=\Phi\Phi^{\top}
has
\mathrm{rank}(K)\leq\min\{n,d\}
and its spectral scale satisfies
\mathrm{Tr}(\tfrac{1}{n}K)=\tfrac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}K_{ii}=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\|\phi(x_{i})\|_{2}^{2}
. If imposing normalization
\|\phi(x)\|_{2}=1
, then the total energy (sum of eigenvalues), which equals to trace, is 1, and increasing
d
would spread energy into different directions; if embedding norm increases along with
d
then the spectrum will be scaled.

Also note that despite we work with vRKHS, the definition of vRKHS should allow a trivialization to scalar case, and therefore could be estimated using above method.
One may also follow the canonical Mercer Theorem and definition of kernel integral operator in
26
for operator-valued kernel (see Proposition 3 & 4 therein), however it’s mathematically equivalent since they consider the vRKHS as a subspace of
L_{2}
via an inclusion map.

L.2
Settings

We now verify the truncated-spectrum assumption and empirically probe eigenstructure for GNNs in different levels of depth and expressiveness on TUDataset
[
82
]
.
We aim to investigate the following questions:

Q1:
does finite-spectrum assumption hold in practice?

Q2:
how does different levels of complexity, e.g., depth, expressiveness, embedding dimension, for hypothesis functions affect spectrum structures?

Dataset statistics.
[
76
]

We choose three real-world data IMDB-MULTI, NCI1, PROTEINS to compare, whose basic statistics are shown in Table
5
. For those datasets, e.g., IMDB and COLLAB, without node feature, we use node degree binning as categorical feature for nodes.
Two factors affect empirical spectrum estimation: (1) number of data samples
n
, and (2) the dimension
d
of feature map
\phi:\mathcal{G}\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
(for GNNs). For (1), we randomly sample a subset with
n=1000
(IMDB-MULTI),
n=2000
(NCI1),
n=1000
(NCI109). For (2), we use WL subtree kernel directly,
d=\mathcal{O}(hn)
; we use dot product kernel
K(G,G^{\prime})=\langle\phi(G),\phi(G^{\prime})\rangle_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}
with embedding dimension
d=256
for GIN and
d=512
for PPGN.
To calculate the feature map
\phi
, we train GIN on
80\%:20\%
train-val set split over
n
(depends on dataset) samples, using Adam optimizer with epochs 200 and batch size 64; and we train PPGN with the same data split protocol yet defaults hyperparameters (e.g., learning rate, batch size, etc.) in the code base
[
76
]
. No embedding regularization (e.g.
\|\phi(x)\|_{2}\leq 1
) are used for GIN and PPGN.
Finally, eigen-decomposition for Gram matrix after diagonal normalization is conducted to compute empirical spectrum (see last section). For all three methods, we traverse the depth of kernel
h\in\{1,2,3,4,5\}
over seeds
0,1,2,3,4
.
Table
6
reports the estimated truncation dimension
r_{\varepsilon}:=\min\left\{r:\ \frac{\sum_{i>r}\lambda_{i}}{\sum_{i}\lambda_{i}}\leq\varepsilon\right\}
, i.e., the smallest rank such that the tail eigenvalue mass beyond
r
contributes at most
\varepsilon
of total.

L.3
Results

As shown in Table
6
, the two sub-tables reports dimension
r_{\varepsilon}
under different conditions of
\varepsilon=\frac{\sum_{i>r}\lambda_{i}}{\sum_{i}\lambda_{i}}
, i.e., the residual eigenvalues beyond rank
r
over the total eigenvalues: left for
\varepsilon=1\%
and right for
\varepsilon=0.1\%
. In plain words, the minimum rank index
r
such that the energy (eigenvalues) beyond
r
larger than
\epsilon\times\mathrm{total\;energy}
.
Usually a lower effective dimension
d_{\mathrm{eff}}
denotes better generalisation. However, in our bound (see proposition above), a higher truncated eigenvalue (representinig the worst direction in vRKHS ball) indicates a better generalisation, meaning that the eigenvalue curve along rank
i
should be as heavy-tail as possible, seemingly equivalent to a larger eigenvalue. This, at first glance is in contradiction, yet the nature of our assumption is controlling the worst direction and hoping that most eigenvalue concentrated on the top
r
rank. We therefore report the
r_{\epsilon}
above as a substitution to
d_{\mathrm{eff}}
. Observations discussed as follows:

\pm

Valid Finite Spectrum Assumption.
As shown in Table
6
, all methods on all datasets presents a concentrated spectrum pattern such that most eigenvalues concentrate in top
r
directions, indicating our assumption holds in practice. Also, this observation is valid since the
r_{\epsilon=1\%}
are all smaller than
n
(1000 or 2000), and thus the upper limit of
r
. Even if not, the finite embedding dimension
d
for practical graph neural networks would also limit such truncation, and therefore assumptions always hold.
Table
8
further shows scaled results on COLLAB (4K), NCI1 (4K), NCI109 (4K) which also demonstrates valid truncated spectrum assumption.
On both sets we observe higher depth
h
yields higher intrinsic dimension.

Nearly exponential decay.

We observe in Figure
4
that eigenvalues decay at a nearly exponential rate, which aligns with approximation-theoretic predictions
[
13
]
, but is less compatible with polynomial decay rates often assumed in classic analyses
[
22
,
65
,
38
,
29
]
.
To interpret this decay for GIN/PPGN, recall that we use a dot-product kernel

K(G,G^{\prime})=\langle\phi(G),\phi(G^{\prime})\rangle_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}
,
where
\phi(G)\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
is the learned graph embedding.Let
\Phi\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times d}
stack embeddings of
n
graphs as rows, i.e.,
\Phi_{i:}=\phi(G_{i})^{\top}
.Then the Gram matrix used for eigendecomposition is simply

K=\Phi\Phi^{\top}
, meaning
K_{ij}
is the dot product between two embeddings.Equivalently, looking at
K
is the same as looking at the “energy distribution” of the embedding coordinates: the nonzero eigenvalues of
K
match those of
\Phi^{\top}\Phi
(up to a scale), which is exactly the (uncentered) covariance/second-moment matrix of embeddings.Hence, a fast eigenvalue decay says that most embedding variance lies in only a few principal directions, while the remaining directions carry little energy.This is why we view small
r_{\varepsilon}\ll d
as
feature compressibility
: the learned representations behave as if they have a low intrinsic dimension.

Consistent feature compressibility and the effect of embedding dimension.

We further probe compressibility by fixing GIN with depth
h=5
and varying the embedding dimension
d\in\{64,128,256,512,1024\}
, training with an 80%/20% train/val split (hyperparameters selected on the validation set) and evaluating
r_{\varepsilon=1\%}
on the full dataset; results are averaged over 5 seeds.
Table
7
shows that
r_{\varepsilon}
remains finite even as
\varepsilon
decreases (e.g.,
1\%\to 0.1\%
), supporting the truncated-spectrum assumption.
Moreover, we empirically observe that larger
d
often yields smaller
r_{\varepsilon}
(i.e., a more concentrated spectrum).
This trend is not a mathematical necessity, but we provide several explanations as follows:

(A)
Low intrinsic task dimension:
the classification signal may lie in a
k\ll d
subspace; increasing
d
provides extra degrees of freedom that can remain unused, so energy concentrates on the same few principal directions.

(B)
Implicit regularization/optimization bias:
even without explicit embedding normalization, training dynamics (e.g., Adam, early stopping) tend to amplify a small set of discriminative directions while leaving many coordinates near initialization, making the embedding covariance effectively low-rank as
d
grows.

(C)
Energy-thresholded truncation:

r_{\varepsilon}
is defined by a tail-energy criterion
\sum_{i>r}\lambda_{i}/\sum_{i}\lambda_{i}\leq\varepsilon
; if additional dimensions mainly contribute near-zero eigenvalues, the relative tail energy decreases and the threshold is met at a smaller
r
.

Overall, these observations indicate that learned graph representations are highly compressible under our setup, and that increasing
d
mainly adds low-variance directions rather than increasing the intrinsic complexity.

Figure 4
:
Eigenvalues on IMDB-MULTI (1k), NCI1 (2k), PROTEINS (1k).

Figure 5
:
d_{\mathrm{eff}}
on IMDB-MULTI (1k), NCI1 (2k), PROTEINS (1k).

:
Eigenvalues on IMDB-MULTI (4k), NCI1 (4k), PROTEINS (4k).

Figure 7
:
d_{\mathrm{eff}}
on IMDB-MULTI (4k), NCI1 (4k), PROTEINS (4k).

L.4
Others

We briefly discuss why we choose to include parameterization (WL-kernel) and why expressiveness as measure of complexity.

Definition L.1

(Definition 2 & 4 in
98
)
.

Let K be any kernel for graphs, that we will call the base kernel. Then the Weisfeiler-Lehman kernel with
h
iterations with the base kernel K is defined as

k_{WL}^{(h)}(G,G^{\prime})=k(G_{0},G_{0}^{\prime})+k(G_{1},G_{1}^{\prime})+\cdots+k(G_{h},G_{h}^{\prime}),

(305)

where
h
is number of Weisfeiler-Lehman iterations and
\{G_{0},\cdots,G_{h}\}
and
\{G_{0}^{\prime},\cdots,G_{h}^{\prime}\}
are the Weisfeiler-Lehman sequences of
G
and
G^{\prime}
respectively. Equivalently, the Weisfeiler-Lehman subtree kernel on two graphs
G
and
G^{\prime}
with
h
iterations can be also defined as:

k_{WL}^{(h)}(G,G^{\prime})=\langle\phi_{WLsubtree}^{(h)}(G),\phi_{WLsubtree}^{(h)}(G^{\prime})\rangle,

(306)

where
\phi_{WLsubtree}^{(h)}(G)
defines the feature map obtained by
h
Weisfeiler-Lehman iterations.

We present a brief definition for WL kernel as above, however kindly refer to
98
, especially Figure 2 for illustrative example of calculation.
In short, WL kernel aggregates neighbour labels and maintains for each node a multiset. For
h
-depth of kernel then
h
-hop neighbours’ labels would be aggregated into current node. This is essentially the mechanism of message-passing in graph neural network. Although GNNs are usually known to achieve better performance than WL kernel due to learnable mappings, WL kernel however still serves as a strong baseline in certain datasets, and as a function with strict 1-WL expressiveness which is empirically & theoretically proven useful for better architecture design.

In recent years, there has been a prevalent research topic over higher-order GNN, i.e., extending 1-WL to 2-WL or 3-WL regarding the expressiveness, because MPNNs are known at most up to 1-WL and thus unable to distinguish isomorphism graphs. The major difference between higher-order
k
-WL lies in number of nodes to preserve: in 1-WL, we preserve only
n
nodes and for each a multiset; yet in
k
-WL, we preserve a
k
-tuple of nodes and for each a multiset. For more details on Weisfeiler-Lehman and its stories in ML, we refer to
83
. Yet, the challenge primarily lies in computation since the complexity increases to
O(n^{k})
and for large graphs it’s barely possible. Thanks to Maron et al. [7,8,9], who proposed a series of work on networks allowing higher-order tensorization with higher-order expressivity guarantees, we now have PPGN
[
76
]
with up to 3-WL network.

Although higher-order expressiveness, by intuition, empowers GNNs with stronger fitting capabilities, it is still in ambiguity if and when are these higher-order GNNs guaranteed to achieve better performance and robustness under distribution shift. In particular, recent works
[
47
,
68
,
78
]
probe this question on theory, and all of them inevitably rely upon certain distance metric between graphs. This however trigger another line of thinking:
when are we guaranteed to distinguish graphs sampled from RGMs?
This question was preliminarily answered in the long version section below, where we propose to compare the kernel mean embedding of graphs, i.e., via the so-called metric maximum mean discrepancy. We hope to investigate more on illustrating relations between expressiveness and generalisation in RKHS, and how to connect reproducing kernel with graph kernel.

Appendix M
Exp 3: Amplitude

M.1
Simulations

We conduct experiments to illustrate and verify our theoretical findings using synthetic graph data for multi-class classification.
We conduct three sets of experiments to validate implications of our theoretical results, on sample complexity, domain divergence, and the effect of class number.

Data Generation.
For each class, we use an RGM that employs a Gaussian distribution in a 4-dimensional latent space to sample nodes with distinct means
\{m_{j}\}_{j=1}^{C}
and identity covariance matrix.
Each RGM uses either an Erdős-Rényi or
\epsilon
-kernel for adjacency matrix, controlled by their kernel parameters.
We vary the latent positions and kernel parameters for different classes so that graphs from different classes differ in both structures and node features.
The mapping function
f
computes an 8-dimensional feature by padding zeros for extra 4 dimensions.

DA Setting.
To generate the source-domain data, we generate
50
graphs for each class. To construct the target domain, we follow the same sampling protocol, but add a Gaussian mean shift in the latent space for each RGM, i.e.,
m_{j}\to m_{j}+s
, creating shifted latent positions in the target domain.

Model Implementation.
We implement MPNN consisting of 3 hidden layers with a hidden dimension of 16. For training, we tune the learning rate over [1e-3, 3e-4, 1e-4] with a batch size 4 and early stopping at 20 epochs. We observe the best target-domain loss. For each setting, we repeat the training-testing trial for 5 times, and report the mean and variance of target-domain losses.

Figure 8
:
Target-domain test loss under different experimental conditions:
(a)
Graph size (
n
),
(b)
Shift level (
s
). Shaded areas indicate variance around the mean loss.

Sample Complexity.
A main indication of our theoretical result is that DA error depends on graph size, and it reduces as the size increases. To validate this, we fix the class number as
C=3
, domain shit as
s=3.0
for all classes, and use the Erdős-Rényi kernel. Following
110
, we increase the node number when sampling graphs for training, e.g., from
n=50
to
n=150
, and test in target domain with fixed graph size
150
. The target-domain loss is reported in Figure
8
(a). As
n
increases, the decrease of the test loss in target domain coincides with our theoretical result.

Domain Divergence.
Another indication is that DA error reduces as domain divergence in latent space decreases. Specifically, Eq. (
8
) indicates an increase along with Wasserstein 2-distance between source and target latent distributions. Since the Wasserstein 2-distance between two Gaussians
\mu_{1}=\mathcal{N}(m_{1},\Sigma_{1})
and
\mu_{2}=\mathcal{N}(m_{2},\Sigma_{2})
has an analytical form, i.e.,
\mathcal{W}_{2}(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})\propto\left\lVert m_{1}-m_{2}\right\rVert_{2}\propto s
, the shift
s
is thus proportional to Wasserstein distance. Therefore, to validate our domain divergence result, we fix the class number
C=3
, source and target domain graph size n=20, use
\epsilon
-kernel, while vary the shift level
s
. The result is reported in Figure
8
(b). We observe that a larger shift
s
in latent space (equivalently a larger Wasserstein distance) leads to higher testing loss in target domain following a polynomial trend. This matches our theoretical results.

Dataset.

Following general protocols
[

,
74
]
, we use
Mutagenicity
and construct four domains
\{\mathrm{M0},\mathrm{M1},\mathrm{M2},\mathrm{M3}\}
by splitting graphs into equally-sized subsets according to edge-density levels (from denser, e.g., M0, to sparser, e.g., M3).
The resulting shift is a controlled structural change: transferring from
\mathrm{M0}\rightarrow\mathrm{M3}
corresponds to a larger density decrease than
\mathrm{M0}\rightarrow\mathrm{M1}
.
We evaluate all pairwise transfer directions among these domains (12 source
\rightarrow
target pairs).

Task.

For each source
\rightarrow
target pair, we train a graph classifier using labeled graphs from the
source
domain only, and evaluate the trained classifier on labeled graphs from the
target
domain.
No target labels are used for training or adaptation; target labels are used only for evaluation.

Model: GCN.

Given node features
x
, the model applies two GCNConv blocks with ReLU and dropout, followed by global mean pooling and a linear classifier:

\displaystyle x

\displaystyle\leftarrow\mathrm{GCNConv}(x,E),\quad x\leftarrow\mathrm{ReLU}(x),\quad x\leftarrow\mathrm{Dropout}(x),

(307)

\displaystyle z_{G}

\displaystyle\leftarrow\mathrm{MeanPool}(x),

(308)

\displaystyle\hat{y}

\displaystyle\leftarrow\mathrm{Linear}(z_{G}).

(309)

M.2.1
Methods compared

We compare three training methods:

•

GCN (vanilla).
Cross-entropy loss training with no weight decay.

•

GCN + L2.
Standard uniform
\ell_{2}
regularization implemented as Adam weight decay (
weight_decay=1e-4
).

•

GCN + Front (front-heavy).
We add a layerwise-weighted quadratic penalty

\mathcal{L}=\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{CE}}+\lambda_{\mathrm{reg}}\sum_{k=0}^{K}\gamma^{-k}\|W_{k}\|_{F}^{2},

(310)

where
k
indexes the sequence of trainable weight matrices in order (GCN blocks first, classifier last).
We set
\lambda_{\mathrm{reg}}=\texttt{1e-4}
. When
\gamma>1
, earlier layers receive stronger penalty (front-heavy).

•

GCN + Back (back-heavy).
We use a layerwise-weighted quadratic penalty with coefficients that are the mirror of front-heavy:

\mathcal{L}=\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{CE}}+\lambda_{\mathrm{reg}}\sum_{k=0}^{K}\gamma^{\,k-K}\|W_{k}\|_{F}^{2},

(311)

where
k
indexes the sequence of trainable weight matrices in order (GCN blocks first, classifier last).
We set
\lambda_{\mathrm{reg}}=\texttt{1e-4}
. When
\gamma>1
, later layers receive stronger penalty (back-heavy).

Selecting
\gamma
.

For the front-heavy method, we optionally sweep
\gamma\in\{1.0,1.2,1.5,2.0,3.0,4.0\}
and select the best
\gamma
by source-domain validation accuracy.
For each
\gamma
, the model is trained on the source train split, evaluated on the source validation split, and the
\gamma
with the highest validation accuracy is selected.

M.2.2
Training protocol and implementation details

Data splitting.

For each source domain, we randomly split the source dataset into 80% training and 20% validation (seeded permutation).
We train on the training split and use the validation split for early stopping and (for
GCN+Front
)
\gamma
selection.
We evaluate on the entire target domain.

Optimization and early stopping.

We use Adam with learning rate
5e-4
.
For
GCN+L2
, we use Adam weight decay
1e-4
.
For
GCN
,
GCN+Front
, and
GCN+Back
, the optimizer weight decay is set to zero, and the front-/back-heavy penalty is added explicitly to the loss only for
GCN+Front
and
GCN+Back
respectively.
We train up to
800
epochs with patience
100
based on source validation accuracy, however due to early stopping the effective epochs commonly end with less than 200.
We use
ReduceLROnPlateau
with factor
0.5
and minimum LR
1e-5
.
Default batch size is
128
, hidden dimension
128
, dropout
0.2
.

Seeds and reporting.

We run 5 random seeds (
0--4
) for each source
\rightarrow
target pair and each method.
We report mean
\pm
std of target accuracy across seeds.
